\subsection{World-frame tracking by ego- and object-motion decomposition (v46, proposed)}
\label{sec:v46}
\emph{Status: \textbf{deferred}. The first-experiment gate was run (below) and revealed that the drivetrack
far-field error is dominated by a monocular metric-depth \emph{scale} bias --- fixed directly by v40's
depth-backbone swap --- rather than by the lack of a world frame. Triangulation with correct pose is exact
(oracle error $0.000$), but recovering metric scale/pose from monocular geometry fails; only learned,
GT-pose-supervised metric pose would realise the gain. Kept here as a documented design; revisit if v40
plateaus. Built on v39 and would warm-start from it.}

\paragraph{First realization (implemented \& evaluated below): the within-frame axis alone.}
The full world-frame program (pose head, static/moving logits, triangulation, object motion, test-time
optimisation) is what the gate defers. As a first, low-risk step we implement only its \emph{architectural
core} --- the missing \textbf{within-frame} axis --- and measure whether cross-track mixing helps by itself.
On top of the v42 refiner we add a \emph{within-frame Vision-Mamba-3 self-attention} over the $N$ tracked
points of each frame (\texttt{Mamba3SelfAttention}, bidirectional, visibility-masked), then a
zero-initialised per-point depth-correction head, $z\leftarrow z\,e^{\Delta_{\text{wf}}}$, so v46 starts
exactly at v42 and each point's depth correction can now see \emph{all other points in its frame} --- the
cross-track information a per-track SSM never has, and the richer counterpart of v43's masked-mean per-frame
\emph{scalar}. No explicit camera pose or world frame is estimated in this first realization; any
ego-motion-consistent correction must be learned implicitly. Warm-started from v42, DA3-g depth, $20$k
steps.

\paragraph{Result: negative, and an out-of-memory failure on adt.}
The within-frame axis, as a plain per-point mixing bolt-on, \textbf{does not help} --- and worse, it
\textbf{cannot be evaluated on the subset it was designed for}. On the two subsets that ran cleanly it is
flat-to-slightly-worse than v42/v45 on the \emph{same} DA3-g depth and WAFT flow (absolute metric-AJ:
pstudio $0.217$ vs v45 $0.226$; drivetrack $0.165$ vs v45 $0.173$; normalized 3D-AJ essentially unchanged,
Tables~\ref{tab:median-g}--\ref{tab:abs-g}). More seriously, v46 \textbf{ran out of memory on $40$ of the
$50$ adt clips}: the pure-torch \texttt{Mamba3SelfAttention} (the fused TileLang kernel is unavailable on the
$12$\,GB GPU) materialises an $O(F\,N^{2})$ within-frame attention over all $N$ tracked points of every frame
at once, which exceeds $12$\,GB on adt's long ($\sim$300-frame) clips. The reported adt ($0.264$) and mean
($0.215$) are therefore over only the $10$ \emph{shortest} adt clips and are \textbf{not comparable} to
v42--v45; even on those $10$ it trails v45. Net: plain within-frame mixing is both \emph{ineffective} (no
gain where it runs) and \emph{impractical} (OOM exactly on adt, the egocentric subset it was meant to
exploit), so it does \emph{not} close the adt gap to DELTA ($0.324$). \textbf{v45 remains the best DA3-g
pipeline} (mean absolute metric-AJ $0.232$). Why a plain within-frame bolt-on was the wrong lever --- and
what to try instead --- is the subject of \S\ref{sec:error-analysis}.

\paragraph{Motivation.}
Everything up to v39 refines each point's depth \emph{independently, in per-frame camera coordinates}: the
SSM runs over time per track with no cross-track mixing. This is robust---it needs no camera pose, which is
exactly why it survives where the pose-dependent TAPIP3D collapses (\S\ref{sec:sota})---but it leaves
absolute accuracy on the table, most visibly in the \textbf{far field on drivetrack} (absolute-AJ
$\approx0.005$, Table~\ref{tab:abs}), the classic monocular metric-depth collapse: a single ray cannot
recover metre-accurate depth at $30$--$100$\,m.

\paragraph{The other target: the adt gap to DELTA.}
The same missing ingredient explains our largest \emph{relative} shortfall to SOTA. \textbf{adt} is the one
subset where DELTA beats every configuration of ours (Table~\ref{tab:abs-g}: absolute metric-AJ $0.324$
vs.\ our best $0.297$), and \S\ref{sec:sota} explains why: adt is egocentric, so most tracked points are the
\emph{static} scene, and their apparent motion is one \emph{shared camera ego-motion} --- a globally
coherent signal a dense, cross-point, multi-frame tracker (DELTA) can lock onto. Our per-track refiner
cannot: it slides each point's depth along its own ray with \emph{no cross-track mixing}, so it never sees
that the whole scene moves together. v46's within-frame pose head is exactly the missing piece --- it
estimates the shared ego-motion $T_{w\to c}(t)$ from \emph{all} points of a frame and lifts static points
into a consistent world frame, turning adt's coherent ego-motion from a liability into the constraint that
fixes it. The adt gap is therefore \emph{not} a depth or per-frame-scale problem (v42--v45 could not close
it); it is the missing cross-track ego-motion axis, which is what v46 adds.

\paragraph{Idea.}
Lift the tracked points into a shared \emph{world frame} and factor each point's motion into a per-frame
camera pose $T_{w\to c}(t)\in SE(3)$ \emph{shared by all points} and a per-point object-motion residual:
\begin{equation}
  \mathbf{X}^{\text{cam}}_t \;=\; T_{w\to c}(t)\,\bigl(\mathbf{X}^{w}_0 + \Delta^{\text{obj}}_t\bigr),
  \label{eq:worldframe}
\end{equation}
so a genuinely \emph{static} point has $\Delta^{\text{obj}}_t\equiv0$ and a constant world position
$\mathbf{X}^{w}_0$. This buys two things the per-frame formulation cannot:
\begin{enumerate}
  \item \textbf{A free multi-frame constraint.} A static point must be fixed in the world frame, so its
  noisy per-frame monocular depth can be averaged/optimised across frames.
  \item \textbf{Parallax triangulation.} With relative pose known, a static point seen from a moving camera
  can be \emph{triangulated}, which is far more accurate than monocular depth at range. This directly
  attacks the far-field bottleneck, correcting DA3-l's depth-scale bias rather than only refining along the
  ray (\S\ref{sec:v46-gate}).
\end{enumerate}

\paragraph{Architecture (what changes from v35/v39).}
Estimating a \emph{shared} per-frame pose requires aggregating information \emph{across points within a
frame}---precisely the non-causal Vision-Mamba-3 cross-attention derived in the backbone but unused by the
per-track temporal refiner. v46 adds a \emph{within-frame} mixing stage (bidirectional Vision-Mamba-3
self-attention over the $N$ points of each frame, order-agnostic, linear in $N$) after the existing
across-frame causal Vision-Mamba-3 refiner, giving the two-axis operator \emph{within-frame non-causal $\times$ across-frame
causal}. On top of the pooled within-frame features it adds three zero-initialised heads so v46 starts
exactly at v39: (i) a per-frame \textbf{camera-pose} head ($6$D rotation $+$ translation $\to$ identity at
init); (ii) a per-point \textbf{moving/static} logit; (iii) a per-point \textbf{depth log-variance}, i.e.\ each
point predicts its \emph{own} uncertainty. This turns the plain $\ell_1$ position loss into an
uncertainty-weighted loss --- formally the negative log-likelihood (NLL) of a Laplace distribution, the
distribution whose NLL \emph{is} the $\ell_1$ loss --- in which each point's error is divided by its own
predicted uncertainty, so the model automatically down-weights unreliable far-field depth.

\paragraph{Difference from the early v1--v32 tracker (\S\ref{sec:early-tracker}).}
v46 and the failed v1--v32 tracker use the \emph{same} two-axis Vision-Mamba-3 operator --- non-causal
within-frame self-attention $\times$ causal across-frame cross-attention. What differs is the \emph{framing},
and that is the whole reason one collapsed and the other should not. v1--v32 regressed the 3D tracks
\emph{end-to-end} from images: with no geometric anchor, ``predict near-static tracks'' was a competitive
local optimum of the loss, and the model collapsed into it (\S\ref{sec:prehistory}). v46 instead runs the
identical operator \emph{additively on a frozen geometric backbone} --- WAFT flow (2D) $+$ metric depth $+$
the v35 refiner --- and lets the within-frame stage estimate only the \emph{residual} that backbone cannot:
the shared ego-motion and a per-point object-motion correction. Its pose/motion heads are zero-initialised
(v46 starts exactly at v39) and gated to fall back to the per-frame estimate, so ``predict zero motion'' is
no longer a collapse but simply the v39 baseline it refines. In one line: the early tracker used the operator
to \emph{regress geometry from scratch}; v46 uses it to \emph{refine geometry} on top of frozen flow and
depth.

\paragraph{Three supporting components.}
\begin{itemize}
  \item \textbf{Rigid motion grouping.} Points on one rigid object share an $SE(3)$; cluster points by
  motion affinity and estimate a per-group residual (differentiable weighted Procrustes), so a point with
  bad depth is corrected by its rigid neighbours.
  \item \textbf{Depth uncertainty.} The log-variance head (above) weights each point's contribution and
  lets multi-frame fusion trust parallax over monocular depth where the latter is unreliable.
  \item \textbf{Test-time optimisation.} A short per-clip optimisation of the sparse world positions and
  poses under a reprojection $+$ static-world-consistency objective.
\end{itemize}

\paragraph{Pose supervision.}
Drivetrack ships per-frame ground-truth camera extrinsics ($\texttt{extrinsics\_w2c}$), which we use to
supervise the pose head and to derive self-supervised static/dynamic targets (a static point's world
position is constant); adt and Panoptic Studio have no pose, so there the pose is self-supervised through a
reprojection $+$ robust static-point Procrustes objective.

\paragraph{Keeping it robust (avoiding TAPIP3D's failure).}
World-frame reasoning reintroduces the very pose dependence that broke TAPIP3D on this hardware. We keep
v46 \emph{additive} on top of v39 with a confidence gate: when the confidently-static set is too small
or the scene is too rotational (egocentric ADT), v46 falls back to the v39 per-frame estimate. Poses are
estimated \emph{robustly from confidently-static points} (RANSAC-weighted alignment), never by trusting raw
monocular depth.

\paragraph{First-experiment gate.}
\label{sec:v46-gate}
Before any training we test the load-bearing claim (parallax triangulation fixes far-field depth) directly
on drivetrack: using exact ground-truth pixel correspondences, compare (a) monocular DA3-depth unprojection
against (b) DLT triangulation across a widened baseline, run with both the ground-truth pose (oracle) and a
pose estimated from the static set, stratified by ground-truth depth (near/mid/far). If estimated-pose
triangulation cuts far-field ($>30$\,m) metric error materially, the world-frame program is justified;
otherwise the bottleneck is elsewhere (front-end depth) and we would instead prioritise the
uncertainty/temporal-fusion route.

\subsection{Error analysis, and the refinements it proposed (v45--v46)}
\label{sec:error-analysis}
The v46 negative result prompts a per-stage question: of our three pipeline processes --- frozen 2D flow
(WAFT), frozen metric depth (DA3-g), and the learned refiner --- \emph{which one carries the remaining
error, on which subset?} We read this off the real evaluation numbers already in hand, using v40 (WAFT $+$
DA3-g, \emph{no} refiner) to isolate the raw-depth stage and v42/v45 to isolate the refiner's contribution
(absolute metric-AJ / real 3D error in metres, mean over visible points):

\begin{table}[h]
\centering
\small
\caption{Per-process error localisation on DA3-g. For every subset two quantities are reported:
\textbf{AJ} $=$ absolute metric-AJ (higher is better) and \textbf{err} $=$ the real 3D position error in
metres, averaged over visible points (lower is better). The 2D-flow process contributes negligible error ---
the tracked pixel is frozen and the 2D reprojection is exact by construction ($|\Delta\mathbf{u}|\!\approx\!0$),
so all remaining error is \emph{depth along the ray}. The raw-depth stage (v40) dominates, overwhelmingly on
drivetrack far field ($4.36$\,m); the refiner (v42$\to$v45) helps everywhere but cannot recover metre-accurate
depth at range.}
\label{tab:error-stage}
\begin{tabular}{lcccccc}
\toprule
 & \multicolumn{2}{c}{drivetrack} & \multicolumn{2}{c}{pstudio} & \multicolumn{2}{c}{adt} \\
\cmidrule(lr){2-3}\cmidrule(lr){4-5}\cmidrule(lr){6-7}
Process (cumulative) & AJ$\uparrow$ & err\,(m)$\downarrow$ & AJ$\uparrow$ & err\,(m)$\downarrow$ & AJ$\uparrow$ & err\,(m)$\downarrow$ \\
\midrule
frozen WAFT 2D flow            & \multicolumn{6}{c}{2D reprojection $\approx$ exact ($|\Delta\mathbf{u}|\!\approx\!0$\,px); no 3D error of its own} \\
$+$ DA3-g raw depth (v40)      & $0.146$ & $4.36$ & $0.192$ & $0.83$ & $0.285$ & $0.42$ \\
$+$ refiner (v42)              & $0.169$ & $3.66$ & $0.213$ & $0.76$ & $0.292$ & $0.39$ \\
$+$ de-flicker (v45, best)     & $0.173$ & $3.64$ & $0.226$ & $0.66$ & $0.297$ & $0.37$ \\
\midrule
DELTA $+$ DA3-g (reference)    & $0.164$ & --- & $0.194$ & --- & $\mathbf{0.324}$ & --- \\
\bottomrule
\end{tabular}
\end{table}

Three facts stand out. \textbf{(1) The flow process is not the bottleneck}: the 2D track is frozen and the
reprojection is exact by construction, which is the whole point of the depth-only design (\S\ref{sec:v33});
all error lives in depth along the ray. \textbf{(2) The dominant absolute error is drivetrack far-field
depth}: the raw-depth 3D error is $4.36$\,m on drivetrack versus $0.4$--$0.8$\,m elsewhere, and even our best
refiner only trims it to $3.64$\,m --- an along-ray refiner slides depth but cannot recover metre-accurate
\emph{scale} at $30$--$100$\,m from a single ray. \textbf{(3) The adt shortfall to DELTA is not a large
per-stage error but an architectural one}: our adt error is already small ($0.37$\,m) and our metric-AJ
($0.297$) is close to DELTA's ($0.324$); the gap is that DELTA exploits the globally shared ego-motion of
adt's static scene, a cross-track constraint our per-track refiner --- and, as v46 showed, a naive per-point
within-frame mixer --- does not capture.

\subsection{Refinements targeting the localised errors (v47)}
\label{sec:v47}
The error analysis of \S\ref{sec:error-analysis} points to three concrete refinements, grouped here as
\textbf{v47}, each naming the dominant error it targets:
\begin{enumerate}
  \item \textbf{Memory-efficient within-frame mixing} (enables everything else). Recompute the within-frame
  Vision-Mamba-3 attention in \emph{frame chunks} (peak memory $O(C\,N^{2})$ for chunk $C\!\ll\!F$ instead of
  $O(F\,N^{2})$), or build the fused TileLang kernel. \emph{Targets:} the v46 OOM --- without it the
  within-frame / world-frame idea cannot even be measured on adt, the subset it exists for. Cheap,
  inference-only, no retraining of the depth/flow front-ends.
  \item \textbf{Far-field depth by multi-frame parallax} (the deferred world-frame program,
  \S\ref{sec:v46}), now re-motivated by Table~\ref{tab:error-stage}: drivetrack far-field depth is
  \emph{the} dominant real error ($3.6$\,m). The v46 gate already showed triangulation with correct pose is
  exact (oracle error $0.000$); the missing piece is a \emph{learned, GT-pose-supervised} metric camera-pose
  head (drivetrack ships GT extrinsics). Lift static far points into a shared world frame and triangulate
  across the widened baseline instead of trusting single-ray monocular depth. \emph{Targets:} the largest
  available absolute-metric win.
  \item \textbf{Shared ego-motion pose head with static-world consistency} (not per-point mixing).
  Estimate \emph{one} per-frame camera pose from a masked-mean pool over all points of a frame ($6$D pose
  head, zero-init to identity) and enforce that static points are constant in the world frame --- exactly the
  coherent-ego-motion signal DELTA exploits. \emph{Targets:} the adt gap to DELTA. v46 is the evidence that
  the plain per-point within-frame axis is the wrong lever for this; the shared-pose formulation is the
  right one.
\end{enumerate}
All three converge on the world-frame decomposition already documented (and gated) in \S\ref{sec:v46}: the
v46 negative result does not retire that program --- it sharpens it, and adds the memory fix as the enabling
first step.

\paragraph{First realization (implemented \& evaluated below): the shared ego-motion pose head.}
We implement refinement~3, the most principled of the three (v46 showed the plain per-point mixer is the
wrong lever). On top of v45 we add a per-frame \emph{masked-mean pool} over the $N$ points of each frame ---
crucially $O(N)$, not $O(F\,N^{2})$, so it \emph{avoids the v46 out-of-memory failure entirely}. The pooled
per-frame feature drives two zero-initialised heads: (i) a \textbf{shared-context depth correction}
$z\!\leftarrow\!z\,e^{\Delta_{\text{ctx}}}$ broadcast back to every point (this is the output-affecting part,
the $O(N)$ counterpart of v46's within-frame mixing), and (ii) a per-frame \textbf{6-DoF camera pose}
$T_{w\to c}(t)$ (6-D-rotation $+$ translation, zero-init $\to$ identity). A \emph{self-supervised
static-world-consistency} loss trains the pose: for the most-static half of points in a clip (lowest
camera-frame speed), their world positions $\mathbf{X}^{w}=R^{\top}(\mathbf{X}^{\text{cam}}-\mathbf{t})$
should be constant across frames; we penalise their temporal variance. The predicted depth is
\emph{detached} in this term, so it trains only the pose head and the shared representation --- never
distorting the depth output --- and at init ($R\!=\!I,\mathbf{t}\!=\!0$) it is already non-zero under real
ego-motion, driving the pose to explain it. Both new heads are zero-init so v47 starts exactly at v45;
warm-started from v45, DA3-g depth, $0.87$\,M trainable, $20$k steps. (This first realization keeps the pose
\emph{self-supervised} on all subsets --- the loader does not expose the drivetrack GT extrinsics; GT-pose
supervision on drivetrack is the natural next increment.)

\paragraph{Result: a clean run, but essentially a wash.}
Unlike v46, v47 evaluates cleanly on \emph{all} $150$ clips ($0$ failures --- the $O(N)$ pool has none of
v46's out-of-memory problem, confirming the memory fix). But the shared ego-motion pose head \textbf{does not
move the needle}, and in particular \textbf{does not improve adt} --- the very subset it was designed for:
\begin{center}\small
\begin{tabular}{lcccc|cccc}
\toprule
 & \multicolumn{4}{c|}{normalized 3D-AJ $\uparrow$} & \multicolumn{4}{c}{absolute metric-AJ $\uparrow$} \\
Method & dt & ps & adt & mean & dt & ps & adt & mean \\
\midrule
v45 (best DA3-g)        & 0.118 & 0.029 & 0.118 & 0.088 & \textbf{0.173} & 0.226 & 0.297 & \textbf{0.232} \\
v46 (within-frame)      & 0.117 & 0.030 & 0.076$^{\dagger}$ & 0.074$^{\dagger}$ & 0.165 & 0.217 & 0.264$^{\dagger}$ & 0.215$^{\dagger}$ \\
v47 (shared pose head)  & 0.120 & 0.033 & 0.119 & \textbf{0.091} & 0.169 & \textbf{0.227} & 0.297 & 0.231 \\
\bottomrule
\end{tabular}
\end{center}
On the \emph{absolute} metric v47 is statistically identical to v45 (mean $0.231$ vs $0.232$; adt $0.2970$
vs $0.2969$ --- unchanged to three decimals) and does not narrow the gap to DELTA's adt ($0.324$). It edges
v45 by a hair on the \emph{normalized} leaderboard mean ($0.091$ vs $0.088$, from small pstudio/adt gains)
and on absolute pstudio ($0.227$ vs $0.226$), but loses the same hair on absolute drivetrack. Net: the
self-supervised static-world-consistency signal trains (the pose head is non-trivial), but a pooled pose
context added on top of the frozen depth is \emph{too weak a lever} to convert adt's shared ego-motion into
better metric depth --- consistent with the error analysis (\S\ref{sec:error-analysis}): the adt gap is
architectural, and closing it needs a stronger mechanism than a self-supervised auxiliary --- GT-pose
supervision on drivetrack and explicit parallax triangulation (refinements~2--3), not a bolt-on pose head.
\textbf{v45 remains the best DA3-g pipeline} (absolute mean $0.232$).

\subsection{Vision-Mamba-3 appearance features without DINOv3 (v48/v49)}
\label{sec:v4849}
Every refiner so far (v33--v47) draws its per-point appearance features from a \emph{frozen, off-the-shelf}
DINOv3 ViT-S backbone; the Vision-Mamba-3 contribution lived only in the SSM refiner core, never in the
feature extractor itself. \textbf{v48} and \textbf{v49} close that gap: they replace the frozen DINOv3
feature encoder with a \emph{trainable Vision-Mamba-3 (NC-SSD) pyramid encoder learned from scratch}, so the
appearance features that condition the depth-along-ray refiner are themselves produced by genuine
Vision-Mamba-3 attention. This lets the evaluation report the standalone ability of real Vision-Mamba-3
features rather than borrowing DINOv3's. Everything else is held fixed at the best existing pipelines: the
depth-along-ray refiner core, the loss, and the WAFT flow $+$ DA3 metric-depth front-end are identical to
v39 (\S\ref{sec:waft}) and v45 (\S\ref{sec:v45}).

\paragraph{What changes.} Only the feature encoder: from the frozen DINOv3 backbone to a NC-SSD
\emph{pyramid encoder} with feature dimension $384$ (matched to DINOv3-S so the feature-projection and the
warm-start align), $6$ heads, $2$ blocks, patch $14$ over a $32$-grid ($448$\,px encoder input --- the same
resolution DINOv3 saw). The refiner (SSM core $+$ read-out heads) is warm-started from the v35 DA3-l
checkpoint (\S\ref{sec:v35}); the Vision-Mamba-3 encoder is \emph{not} in that checkpoint and so starts from
random init. Because the encoder is now trained rather than frozen, the trainable-parameter count rises from
the $0.40$\,M refiner-only budget to $1.87$\,M (the extra ${\sim}1.5$\,M is the Vision-Mamba-3 encoder).
Same recipe otherwise: $20$k steps, AdamW, lr $3\!\times\!10^{-4}$, warm-up $500$, $8$-frame windows, bf16
($\sim$$14$\,h each on one RTX~4080 Laptop GPU).

\paragraph{Two depth backbones.} \textbf{v48} keeps DA3-l depth --- the direct analog of v39 (WAFT flow $+$
DA3-l $+$ refiner) --- while \textbf{v49} uses DA3-g depth, the analog of v45. Each is compared against its
DINOv3-feature predecessor at the \emph{same} depth backbone, so the difference isolates the feature
extractor alone.

\paragraph{Result: from-scratch Vision-Mamba-3 features do not yet match frozen DINOv3.}
Both variants train and evaluate cleanly on all $150$ clips. On the \emph{absolute} metric --- the one this
work cares about --- replacing DINOv3 with a from-scratch Vision-Mamba-3 encoder \textbf{costs} accuracy on
every subset and both depth backbones: v48 lowers the DA3-l mean from v39's $0.246$ to $0.205$ ($-0.041$),
and v49 lowers the DA3-g mean from v45's $0.232$ to $0.208$ ($-0.024$).
\begin{center}\small
\begin{tabular}{lcccc|cccc}
\toprule
 & \multicolumn{4}{c|}{normalized 3D-AJ $\uparrow$} & \multicolumn{4}{c}{absolute metric-AJ $\uparrow$} \\
Feature encoder & dt & ps & adt & mean & dt & ps & adt & mean \\
\midrule
\multicolumn{9}{l}{\emph{DA3-l depth}}\\
\quad v39, DINOv3 (frozen)          & \textbf{0.098} & \textbf{0.073} & \textbf{0.151} & \textbf{0.107} & \textbf{0.141} & \textbf{0.279} & \textbf{0.318} & \textbf{0.246} \\
\quad v48, Vision-Mamba-3 (scratch) & \textbf{0.098} & 0.047 & 0.125 & 0.090 & 0.134 & 0.196 & 0.286 & 0.205 \\
\midrule
\multicolumn{9}{l}{\emph{DA3-g depth}}\\
\quad v45, DINOv3 (frozen)          & \textbf{0.118} & 0.029 & 0.118 & 0.088 & \textbf{0.173} & \textbf{0.226} & \textbf{0.297} & \textbf{0.232} \\
\quad v49, Vision-Mamba-3 (scratch) & \textbf{0.118} & \textbf{0.039} & \textbf{0.123} & \textbf{0.093} & 0.146 & 0.192 & 0.285 & 0.208 \\
\bottomrule
\end{tabular}
\end{center}
The gap is uniform on the absolute metric but much smaller --- and on DA3-g even mildly reversed --- on the
\emph{normalized} leaderboard: v49 edges v45 on normalized mean ($0.093$ vs $0.088$, from small pstudio/adt
gains) and v48 ties v39 on drivetrack. The reading is consistent: DINOv3's large-scale pretraining buys
appearance features that a $1.5$\,M-parameter encoder trained from scratch on only $4418$ clips cannot yet
match, and what it loses concentrates in the \emph{absolute-scale-relevant} detail (relative/normalized
shape is nearly preserved). Genuine Vision-Mamba-3 features are therefore a viable but currently
\emph{lossy} drop-in for DINOv3 in the refiner; closing the ${\sim}0.02$--$0.04$ absolute-AJ gap would need
either a larger or pretrained Vision-Mamba-3 encoder, or joint (rather than from-scratch) feature training.
\textbf{The DINOv3-feature pipelines --- v39 on DA3-l, v45 on DA3-g --- remain best on the absolute metric.}

\subsection{The VSSD-2pool mixer in the refiner (v50/v51)}
\label{sec:v50-twopool}

The refiner mixes each track over time with \texttt{Mamba3CrossAttention(variant="B")}, whose
mask $L[i,j]=\gamma_j\prod_{k=j+1}^{T}\alpha_k$ keeps only the $\gamma$ band and is \emph{rank-1
across the query axis}: every query row is identical, so one pool applies the same weighting over
the track to every frame. This replaces it with the VSSD-2pool construction --- a
second, independently-read pool with its own query projection $C^{(2)}$ and its own per-token
vector $m^{(2)}$ --- which lifts that restriction. The second pool's contribution is scaled by a
parameter initialised to zero, so a two-pool layer is numerically identical to a one-pool layer at
initialisation (verified: $\max|\Delta|=0$).

\paragraph{Why ``2-pool'' and not ``VSSD-$\beta,\gamma$''.} The operator was previously named
after the bands of the Mamba-3 mask its pools were meant to carry: VSSD-$\gamma$ for the single
pool and VSSD-$\beta,\gamma$ for the pair. That name claims more than we can show. The collapse
sets $m_j = 1/A_j$, which is a reinterpretation of the state transition, not literally
$\gamma_j = \lambda_j\Delta_j$; and the second pool's $m^{(2)}$ is a freely-learned per-token
vector that is never constrained to equal $\beta$. The companion note says as much --- the second
pool is ``a motivated capacity extension, not a proof that it reproduces Mamba-3's causal
$\beta$-band behaviour'' --- so the band-based name asserts a correspondence the text itself
declines to make. \textbf{VSSD-1pool} and \textbf{VSSD-2pool} instead name the construction: how
many independently-read pools the operator has. That is verifiable from the code, is what
actually distinguishes the two, and carries no claim about which bands survive the collapse.

\paragraph{Cold start, deliberately.} Fine-tuning the two-pool model from the v35 checkpoint is
cheaper and confounded: it would have seen $20$k one-pool steps \emph{plus} the fine-tune against
v35's $20$k, so a gain could be the extra training rather than the second pool. It therefore trains
from scratch for $20$k steps with v35's configuration and data, \texttt{two\_pool} the only
difference. \textbf{v50} denotes that training; \textbf{v51} denotes its evaluation under the
WAFT front-end, which is the protocol the reported baseline uses.

\paragraph{Result: the second pool improves the tracker.} Both rows below are the same refiner
evaluated identically --- WAFT 2-D tracks, DA3-l depth, TAPVid-3D minival --- and differ only in
whether the mixer has one pool or two.

\begin{center}
\begin{tabular}{lcccc}
\toprule
mean metric-AJ & drivetrack & pstudio & adt & mean \\
\midrule
v39 \; one pool (reported)  & $0.1408$ & $0.2787$ & $0.3181$ & $0.2458$ \\
v51 \; two pool             & $\mathbf{0.1467}$ & $\mathbf{0.2999}$ & $\mathbf{0.3214}$ & $\mathbf{0.2560}$ \\
\bottomrule
\end{tabular}
\end{center}

\noindent Ahead on the mean by $0.0102$ and on \emph{all three} subsets. The \texttt{adt} column is
the one that moved: it read $0.3120$ and a loss of $0.0061$ until the two-pool arm was re-scored on
the collapse path (\S\ref{sec:collapse-reeval}), because $23$ \texttt{adt} clips had been dropped by
an out-of-memory failure the one-pool baseline never hit. The secondary metrics move the same way:
normalized 3D-AJ $0.1069\to0.1153$. The cost is $0.442\to0.612$\,M trainable
parameters ($+38\%$), so the gain is real but not free.

\paragraph{How this was nearly reported backwards.} The first evaluation of the v50 checkpoint ran
without \texttt{--waft-pred-dir} and therefore used the SEA-RAFT front-end that \texttt{v35.yaml}
configures, then compared the result against the paper's \emph{WAFT} row. That gave mean $0.2216$
against $0.2458$ and the apparent conclusion that the second pool costs $0.024$ --- comparing a
SEA-RAFT evaluation against a WAFT baseline. The paper reports both front-ends
(SEA-RAFT$+$DA3-l$+$vmamba3 $=0.225$, WAFT$+$DA3-l$+$vmamba3 $=0.246$), so the front-end alone is
worth about $0.021$ on this pipeline. The lesson is the one the ETH3D chase produced: a fresh run
must be compared against a baseline produced under the same configuration, and ``v39'' is an
\emph{evaluation} label for the v35 checkpoint under WAFT, not a separate model --- there is no
v39 config and no v39 training run.

\paragraph{Why the front-end is not retrained.} The refiner does see flow during training
(\texttt{track\_clip} runs inside the training loop), so it learns the front-end's error
characteristics, and training on SEA-RAFT while evaluating on WAFT is a genuine train/test
mismatch. That mismatch is present in the \emph{baseline} too --- $0.2458$ is the SEA-RAFT-trained
v35 checkpoint evaluated with WAFT. Matching it is therefore what makes the comparison
one-variable; retraining on WAFT would make v51 differ from the baseline in two ways instead of
one. A WAFT-trained arm is a worthwhile separate experiment, but it needs its own WAFT-trained
one-pool baseline to be interpretable.

\paragraph{What the number does not yet carry.} Single seed, no variance estimate for the tracker,
and $+0.0071$ is small in absolute terms. Before this is claimed as a headline improvement it
needs either repeat seeds or a statement of what the run-to-run spread is on this benchmark ---
neither of which exists yet.

\paragraph{What was deliberately not changed.} 2-D RoPE is the best CIFAR-10 pairing for this
operator, but its angle $\theta^{\mathrm{row}}[n]\,r+\theta^{\mathrm{col}}[n]\,c$ is a function of
a patch's row and column on an image grid, and this mixer runs over a track's \emph{time} axis,
which has neither. The pinned rotary does not apply either: variant B forms $C^{q}B^{kv\top}$
pairwise before the mask, so absolute angles cancel and there is no pooled sum to decohere --- the
failure pinning repairs cannot occur here. Pinning would additionally normalise by sequence
length, which is wrong when training uses $8$-frame windows and inference uses clips of hundreds
of frames (at $n{=}0.25$ the adjacent-frame angle is $11.3^{\circ}$ at $T{=}8$ but $0.30^{\circ}$
at $T{=}300$). The variant worth trying is an \emph{unpinned} $1$-D temporal rotary, whose
per-frame increment is independent of $T$.

\paragraph{v52.} The same swap on v45 (DA3-g $+$ de-flicker $+$ vmamba3, reported $0.232$) is
configured but unrun; it was started and stopped after $1$\,h\,$11$\,m to free the GPU.

\subsection{The quadratic-path defect, and the re-scoring of every learned row (v35--v51)}
\label{sec:collapse-reeval}

\texttt{Mamba3CrossAttention} has two paths that compute the same function. The \emph{collapse}
path builds the pooled state $h=\sum_j m_j B_j V_j^{\top}$ of size $(N,d_\mathrm{head})$ and reads
it with $C$ --- the VSSD collapse as written, linear in $T$. The \emph{token-level} path instead
materialises the full $T_q\times T_{kv}$ similarity and multiplies it by the expanded mask, at
$O(T^2)$.

The token-level path was the module default, and all three refiner sites inherited it. Nothing
required this: \texttt{build\_cross\_mask} computed a per-token \emph{vector} and then expanded it
across the query axis, its own docstring noting that all $T_q$ rows are identical. The
bidirectional mask is likewise a vector, $\gamma_j(\mathrm{fwd}+\mathrm{rev})$. That vector is now
\texttt{build\_cross\_scale} and both paths consume it, so the collapse path handles bidirectional
directly and no site needs the quadratic one.

\paragraph{What it cost.} Peak memory on the token-level path grows with batch $\times\,T^2$. On
\texttt{adt} --- $T=300$ frames and $679$--$957$ tracked points, against \texttt{pstudio}'s $150$
and $223$ --- one such tensor is already $2.4$\,GB, and the second pool's measured $1.58\times$ took
a $12$\,GB card over. The two-pool arms lost $33$ and $23$ \texttt{adt} clips to out-of-memory
failures; the one-pool arms never hit it and always covered all $25814$ frames. The published
$0.2529$ was therefore scored on $18914$ frames against a $25814$-frame baseline, and the exclusion
was systematic rather than random: every \texttt{adt} clip is $T=300$, so what decided the failure
was track count, meaning the clips with the most tracked points were the ones lost.

\paragraph{The re-scoring.} Every learned row was re-evaluated on the collapse path. The two paths
agree to $2.8$--$3.1\times10^{-6}$ relative across $\{$bidirectional$\}\times\{$two-pool$\}$, and
through the trained v50 weights to $5.1\times10^{-6}$ at $T=300$, so the checkpoints transfer
unchanged. Nothing was retrained; this is a change of evaluation cost, not of function.

\begin{center}
\begin{tabular}{llcc}
\toprule
arm & quantity & before & after \\
\midrule
v35 SEA-RAFT$+$DA3-l$+$vmamba3 & mean metric-AJ & $0.234$ & $0.225$ \\
v39 WAFT$+$DA3-l$+$vmamba3     & mean metric-AJ & $0.2458$ & $0.2458$ \\
v41 WAFT$+$DA3-g$+$vmamba3     & mean metric-AJ & $0.2161$ & $0.2161$ \\
v42 $+$ pstudio oversample     & mean metric-AJ & $0.2246$ & $0.2246$ \\
v43 $+$ scale head             & mean metric-AJ & $0.2292$ & $0.2292$ \\
v44 de-flicker standalone      & mean metric-AJ & $0.2026$ & $0.2026$ \\
v45 de-flicker $+$ refiner     & mean metric-AJ & $0.2320$ & $0.2320$ \\
\midrule
v50 SEA-RAFT$+$2pool & mean metric-AJ (frames) & $0.222$ ($15914$) & $\mathbf{0.235}$ ($25814$) \\
v51 WAFT$+$2pool     & mean metric-AJ (frames) & $0.253$ ($18914$) & $\mathbf{0.256}$ ($25814$) \\
\bottomrule
\end{tabular}
\end{center}

\noindent Six of the seven complete arms reproduce to four decimals, which is the expected result
and the reason the sweep was worth running rather than assumed. Only the two-pool rows moved, and
only through \texttt{adt}: \texttt{drivetrack} and \texttt{pstudio} shifted by at most $0.0002$,
because those subsets never lost a clip.

\paragraph{v35 does not reconcile, and is recorded as measured.} The $0.234$ previously carried for
v35 is not reproducible. Only four evaluations ever used that checkpoint --- $0.2458$ (WAFT),
$0.2263$ (the \texttt{v36\_fuse} directory), $0.2255$ (this re-run) and $0.0034$ (drivetrack only)
--- and none yields $0.234$. The disagreement is confined to \texttt{pstudio} ($0.274$ against
$0.248$); \texttt{drivetrack} and \texttt{adt} match exactly. The re-run used the default
front-end, which is v35's configuration rather than v36's \texttt{--bidir-fuse}, yet lands on the
v36 row. The tables now carry the measured $0.225$. This is a flow-chaining provenance gap, not an
operator one --- every arm that shares the operator reproduced exactly.

\paragraph{Throughput.} On the collapse path the second pool costs nothing measurable. Matched
protocol, all $25814$ frames: WAFT $41.20$\,fps one-pool against $41.58$ two-pool, SEA-RAFT $6.72$
against $6.92$ --- against the $1.58\times$ memory and the out-of-memory failures the token-level
path produced.

% =====================================================================
\subsection{The SEA-RAFT-trained, WAFT-evaluated rows (v35--v51)}
\label{sec:searaft-trained-waft-eval}

Every WAFT row recorded before v57 was \emph{trained} with the SEA-RAFT front-end and only
\emph{evaluated} on WAFT. The refiner sees flow during training --- \texttt{track\_clip} runs inside
the training loop --- so it learns one front-end's error characteristics and is then scored on a
different one. That is a train/test mismatch, and those numbers are superseded by the WAFT-trained
arms v57--v62. They are kept here, and only here: the paper carries the corrected numbers alone,
because a manuscript reports what the method does, not the order in which it was measured.

\begin{center}
\begin{tabular}{llcc}
\toprule
row & front-end at train / eval & mean metric-AJ & status \\
\midrule
v35 & SEA-RAFT / SEA-RAFT & $0.225$ & matched, superseded by v57 \\
v39 & SEA-RAFT / WAFT     & $0.2458$ & \emph{mismatched} \\
v50 & SEA-RAFT / SEA-RAFT & $0.235$ & matched, superseded by v58 \\
v51 & SEA-RAFT / WAFT     & $0.2560$ & \emph{mismatched} \\
\midrule
v45 & SEA-RAFT / WAFT     & $0.2320$ & \emph{mismatched}; see \S\ref{sec:waft-deflicker-silent} \\
\bottomrule
\end{tabular}
\end{center}

\noindent The mismatched rows score \emph{higher} than their matched counterparts --- $0.2458$
against $0.225$ for one pool, $0.2560$ against $0.235$ for two --- which is why the defect was not
obvious from the numbers. WAFT is the stronger front-end, and swapping it in at evaluation time
improves the input the refiner is given without the refiner having been trained for it. The gain is
real but it is not the gain a WAFT pipeline would deliver, and it cannot be attributed to the
refiner: the arm changed two things at once. v57--v62 hold the front-end fixed at WAFT for both
training and evaluation, which is the only configuration that answers ``how good is this pipeline
on WAFT''.

\paragraph{Two further defects in the same rows.} They were also produced on the token-level
attention path (\S\ref{sec:collapse-reeval}), and their evaluation tracks came from a 2026-07-09
cache built under a different cuDNN configuration. v57--v62 use the collapse path throughout and
regenerate both the training and the evaluation tracks from one code path.

%% ================================================================
\subsection{The WAFT-trained arms, and what the regression turned out to be (v57--v62)}
\label{sec:waft-trained-arms}

v57--v60 hold the flow front-end fixed at WAFT for \emph{both} training and evaluation, which is the
configuration \S\ref{sec:searaft-trained-waft-eval} called for. All completed 2026-08-21 over all
$25814$ minival frames with zero failures. v62, added later, is the arm that identified the cause.

\begin{center}
\begin{tabular}{llccccc}
\toprule
arm & depth backbone & de-flicker & pools & trained on & mean metric-AJ \\
\midrule
v39 & DA3-l & --- & 1 & SEA-RAFT & $0.2459$ \\
v57 & DA3-l & --- & 1 & WAFT     & $\mathbf{0.2504}$ \\
v58 & DA3-l & --- & 2 & WAFT     & $0.2507$ \\
\midrule
v42 & DA3-g & no  & 1 & SEA-RAFT & $0.2246$ \\
v45 & DA3-g & yes & 1 & SEA-RAFT & $\mathbf{0.2320}$ \\
v59 & DA3-g & yes & 1 & WAFT     & $0.2194$ \\
v60 & DA3-g & yes & 2 & WAFT     & $0.2211$ \\
v62 & DA3-g & \textbf{no} & 2 & WAFT & $0.2228$ \\
\bottomrule
\end{tabular}
\end{center}

\noindent Every baseline was re-scored on the same evaluation tracks as the arm it is compared
against, so none of these differences is a track artefact: v45 gave $0.2320$ on both the old and the
regenerated tracks, and v39 gave $0.2459$ against its published $0.2458$.

\noindent\emph{Superseded as a front-end comparison.} Every row above scores a WAFT-cached
evaluation, and the SEA-RAFT rows were trained with SEA-RAFT and then scored that way, so those
comparisons mix front-ends across training and evaluation. The table remains an accurate record of
what was measured, and the reasoning that follows from it is what located the cause, but the
front-end question it appears to answer is settled only under the protocol of
\S\ref{sec:waft-matched-protocol}, where both arms run live at one resolution and one iteration
count.

\subsubsection{WAFT training does not hurt the refiner; it costs the de-flicker stage's contribution}
\label{sec:waft-deflicker-silent}

The first reading of these numbers was that WAFT training makes the pipeline worse, which cannot be
right for a stronger front-end. Splitting by backbone shows it is not what happens.

\begin{itemize}
  \item On \textbf{DA3-l}, which has no de-flicker stage, WAFT training \emph{helps}:
    $0.2459\to0.2504$, $+0.0045$.
  \item On \textbf{DA3-g without de-flicker}, it is neutral: $0.2246\to0.2228$, $-0.0018$.
  \item On \textbf{DA3-g with de-flicker}, it appears to hurt: $0.2320\to0.2194$, $-0.0126$.
\end{itemize}

\noindent The difference is entirely in what the de-flicker stage contributes:
%
\[
  \text{SEA-RAFT-trained: } 0.2246 \to 0.2320 \;(+0.0074), \qquad
  \text{WAFT-trained: } 0.2228 \to 0.2211 \;(-0.0017).
\]
%
The stage is worth $+0.0074$ when the refiner is trained on SEA-RAFT and nothing at all when it is
trained on WAFT. It is not harming the pipeline --- removing it entirely (v62) recovers only
$+0.0017$ --- it has stopped working, and the contribution it used to make is what the $-0.0126$ is
made of.

A direct measurement of the stage agrees. Run on identical inputs, its per-frame log-scale output is
$0.0006$ in the WAFT-trained model against $0.0191$ in v45, a thirty-fold reduction: a zero-initialised
head that has stayed at zero.

\paragraph{A candidate mechanism, not yet established.} The de-flicker estimates one scalar per frame
by aggregating over the points marked \emph{visible} in that frame. WAFT declares $68.5\%$ of
point-frames visible against SEA-RAFT's $85.6\%$ --- and is far more accurate in doing so, agreeing
with ground truth $97.0\%$ of the time against $79.3\%$. The aggregate is therefore built from about
a fifth fewer points, which is the condition under which a zero-initialised head learns to stay near
zero. This is consistent with every measurement here but has not been tested.

\subsubsection{The cause: the cached-track path silently removes an augmentation the live path applies}
\label{sec:waft-aug-asymmetry}

The reasoning that found this is worth stating first, because it is what made the search finite.
\emph{At evaluation both models receive the same WAFT input.} The evaluation front-end, its
positions and its visibility flags are byte-identical for v45 and v59; only the weights differ. So
no property of WAFT at inference can explain the gap, and every candidate that lived at evaluation
time --- the track files, the depth distribution, visibility, flicker --- was eliminated in turn
(\S\ref{sec:waft-eliminated}). What remains is training. And a model trained on the \emph{matched}
front-end cannot legitimately lose to one trained on a \emph{mismatched} one; if it does, the
training procedures were not equivalent.

\paragraph{The models disagree about which data is hard.} Running both checkpoints over
\emph{training} clips, on identical batches, gives the opposite ranking to minival:

\begin{center}
\begin{tabular}{lcc}
\toprule
model & 3-D error on \emph{training} clips & mean metric-AJ on minival \\
\midrule
v45 (SEA-RAFT-trained) & $0.8585$\,m & $\mathbf{0.2320}$ \\
v59 (WAFT-trained)     & $\mathbf{0.7034}$\,m & $0.2194$ \\
\bottomrule
\end{tabular}
\end{center}

\noindent v59 is markedly better on the data it was trained on and worse on data it has not seen.
That is overfitting, and it points at the training procedure rather than at the front-end.

\paragraph{Where the procedures diverge.} Training runs with \texttt{augment=True}, which applies a
photometric jitter --- brightness and contrast each drawn from $[0.7,1.3]$ --- to the window's
images. Both arms receive those recoloured images for the DINOv3 appearance feature. The 2-D track
is where they part:

\begin{itemize}
  \item The \textbf{SEA-RAFT} branch runs \texttt{track\_clip} on \texttt{batch.images}, i.e.\ on
    the \emph{recoloured} frames, at every step. A different colour draw yields a slightly different
    flow field and therefore a slightly different tracked position.
  \item The \textbf{WAFT} branch reads a track computed once, offline, from the \emph{original}
    frames. Recolouring an image today cannot alter a number already written to disk.
\end{itemize}

\noindent Measured on the same window and the same queries, with only the photometric draw changed:

\begin{center}
\begin{tabular}{lcccc}
\toprule
front-end & median $|\Delta \mathbf{u}|$ & mean & p90 & unchanged \\
\midrule
SEA-RAFT & $0.1395$\,px & $1.3003$\,px & $4.0565$\,px & $12.5\%$ \\
WAFT (cached) & $0.0000$ & $0.0000$ & $0.0000$ & $\mathbf{100\%}$ \\
\bottomrule
\end{tabular}
\end{center}

\noindent The SEA-RAFT arm therefore trains against a 2-D input that is resampled every epoch, and
the WAFT arm against one that is byte-identical across all $20\,000$ steps. Input noise resampled
each pass is a regulariser; the cached path removes it.

Nobody wrote that regulariser deliberately. Photometric augmentation exists to vary
\emph{appearance}; the jitter in the tracked coordinates is a side effect of recomputing flow on
altered images, and the tell is that the cached path has no equivalent --- had the coordinate jitter
been a designed part of training, the cached path would have needed its own version of it. The
consequence is that ``SEA-RAFT-trained'' and ``WAFT-trained'' were never the same experiment with one
variable changed.

\paragraph{Why not simply add random jitter to the cached tracks.} It would reproduce the
\emph{symptom} --- input that moves each epoch --- without reproducing its cause, and the two are not
the same distribution: the flow's response to a colour change is structured, concentrated where the
image is low-contrast, and correlated along a track, whereas independent Gaussian noise is none of
those. The comparison being made here is between two front-ends under one protocol, so the protocol
has to match in mechanism, not merely in variance.

\paragraph{The correction.} Setting \texttt{flow.source: waft\_live} runs WAFT inside the training
loop on the augmented images, exactly as the SEA-RAFT path runs SEA-RAFT. It is a model swap rather
than a new code path: both front-ends are consumed by the same \texttt{track\_clip}, so once the
cache is out of the picture the flow network is the only difference between the two arms.

The first version of this correction was itself misconfigured, in a way that took a reader's
scepticism to expose, and \S\ref{sec:waft-matched-protocol} records what was wrong and what the arm
measures once it is fixed.


\subsubsection{Removing the cache exposed two further asymmetries, and only then does the arm measure the front-end}
\label{sec:waft-matched-protocol}

Running WAFT live removed the augmentation asymmetry of \S\ref{sec:waft-aug-asymmetry}, and in doing
so made two others visible. Both had been present all along, hidden behind the cache; both made the
two arms differ in something other than the flow network, which is the one thing the comparison is
supposed to isolate.

\paragraph{Resolution: each front-end was running at its own config's default.} Both wrappers take a
base-2 exponent \texttt{scale} and apply it to the input images, \texttt{scale\_factor} $= 2^{\text{scale}}$,
undoing it afterwards on both the flow field and the flow vectors. SEA-RAFT's
\texttt{spring-M.json} sets $-1$; WAFT's \texttt{tar-c-t.json} sets $0$. Neither was ever passed
explicitly; on a $896 \times 896$ input SEA-RAFT therefore ran at $448 \times 448$ and WAFT at the
full $896 \times 896$:

\begin{center}\small
\begin{tabular}{lrrr}
\toprule
front-end & \texttt{scale} & runs at & ViT tokens \\
\midrule
SEA-RAFT (\texttt{spring-M})  & $-1$ & $448\times448$ & $1\,024$ \\
WAFT (\texttt{tar-c-t})       & $0$  & $896\times896$ & $4\,096$ \\
\bottomrule
\end{tabular}
\end{center}

Four times the pixels and four times the tokens, into a backbone that is itself two Vision
Transformers, and $1.9\times$ even WAFT's own $432 \times 960$ training resolution. This is what
produced the claim, recorded here because it was wrong and was repeated more than once, that WAFT is
roughly ten times heavier per step than SEA-RAFT. It is not a property of WAFT; it is a measurement
of two different operating points. WAFT's own paper reports SEA-RAFT~(L) at $108$\,ms against
WAFT-DAv2-a2 at $240$\,ms at matched iterations, and its headline ``up to $4.1\times$ faster'' is an
accuracy-matched claim against other methods, not a latency claim against SEA-RAFT. With
\texttt{flow.scale} set to $-1$ for both, the training rate rose from $3.9$ to $12.5$ steps per
minute on the DA3-g arm, and on the lighter DA3-l arm WAFT runs at $40$ steps per minute, which is
SEA-RAFT's own rate. Even the residual $3.2\times$ is therefore specific to the DA3-g
configuration and does not generalise.

\paragraph{Refinement steps: the config's value reached only one of the two.} The configs set
\texttt{iters: 4}, which was passed to SEA-RAFT and not to WAFT, leaving WAFT on
\texttt{tar-c-t.json}'s default of $5$. The WAFT arm was therefore given $25\%$ more refinement than
the baseline it was being measured against. \texttt{flow.iters} now reaches whichever front-end
\texttt{flow.source} selects.

\paragraph{The evaluator could not run WAFT at all, which is why a cache existed anywhere.} Caching
was never a protocol decision; \texttt{eval\_metric3d} had no WAFT path, and the cache was the way
around that. Regenerating it at the corrected resolution would only have relocated the defect, since
nothing checks a cache against what training saw. The evaluator now builds WAFT through the same
\texttt{build\_flow} and consumes it through the same \texttt{track\_clip} as SEA-RAFT, running it
live on the evaluation frames, and reads \texttt{flow.source}, \texttt{flow.scale} and
\texttt{flow.iters} from the \texttt{cfg.json} written beside the checkpoint. A run is therefore
scored with the front-end it was trained with, at the same resolution and iteration count, without
anyone having to remember to say so.

\paragraph{A protocol defect introduced by editing the code mid-run.} The \texttt{flow.iters}
plumbing landed while the DA3-g arm was training. Training had started before it and therefore used $5$;
the evaluation at the end of the same job started afterwards, read the config, and used $4$. The
run's \texttt{cfg.json} recorded $4$ --- the intention, not the run. It has been corrected to $5$
with a note, and the arm re-evaluated consistently. The difference turned out to be immaterial,
which is worth recording alongside the defect: \texttt{drivetrack} $0.1628 \to 0.1631$,
\texttt{pstudio} $0.2032 \to 0.2027$, \texttt{adt} $0.3053 \to 0.3056$, and the mean unchanged at
$0.2238$. The mismatched run is kept rather than deleted.

\paragraph{What the corrected arm measures.} With WAFT live on both sides at
\texttt{scale}~$=-1$, over all $25\,814$ frames with no failures:

\begin{center}\small
\begin{tabular}{lccc}
\toprule
DA3-g $+$ de-flicker $+$ VSSD-2pool & flow at train & flow at eval & mean metric-AJ \\
\midrule
v60 & WAFT cached, full-res & WAFT cached, full-res & $0.2211$ \\
v63 & WAFT live, half-res   & WAFT live, half-res   & $\mathbf{0.2238}$ \\
\bottomrule
\end{tabular}
\end{center}

Removing the cache is worth $+0.0027$ on this arm. That is a small gain, and smaller than the
$-0.0126$ the cached WAFT arms had fallen behind by; the augmentation asymmetry of
\S\ref{sec:waft-aug-asymmetry} therefore accounts for part of that gap but not all of it.

\paragraph{Every arm, with the resolution each front-end actually ran at.} Resolution is the column
that reconciles the results, and it was absent from every earlier version of this comparison. A
\texttt{scale} of $0$ means the flow network saw the full $896 \times 896$ input; $-1$ means it saw
$448 \times 448$, a quarter of the pixels. At evaluation time there is no augmentation; a cached
track and a live run at the same scale and iteration count are therefore the same thing --- ``cached'' matters
only on the training side, where the images are augmented and the cache is not recomputed.

\begin{center}\small
\begin{tabular}{llccll r}
\toprule
& & & & \multicolumn{1}{c}{train flow} & \multicolumn{1}{c}{eval flow} & \\
arm & depth & pools & de-fl. & \multicolumn{1}{c}{net / res / iters} & \multicolumn{1}{c}{net / res / iters} & mean \\
\midrule
v39 & DA3-l & 1 & ---  & SEA-RAFT $\;448\;$ i4 live   & WAFT $\;896\;$ i5 & $0.2458$ \\
v57 & DA3-l & 1 & ---  & WAFT $\;896\;$ i5 \emph{cached} & WAFT $\;896\;$ i5 & $0.2504$ \\
v58 & DA3-l & 2 & ---  & WAFT $\;896\;$ i5 \emph{cached} & WAFT $\;896\;$ i5 & $0.2507$ \\
v51 & DA3-l & 2 & ---  & SEA-RAFT $\;448\;$ i4 live   & WAFT $\;896\;$ i5 & $\mathbf{0.2560}$ \\
v50 & DA3-l & 2 & ---  & SEA-RAFT $\;448\;$ i4 live   & SEA-RAFT $\;448\;$ i4 & $0.2350$ \\
v64 & DA3-l & 2 & ---  & WAFT $\;448\;$ i4 live       & WAFT $\;448\;$ i4 & $0.2547$ \\
\midrule
v42 & DA3-g & 1 & no   & SEA-RAFT $\;448\;$ i4 live   & WAFT $\;896\;$ i5 & $0.2246$ \\
v45 & DA3-g & 1 & yes  & SEA-RAFT $\;448\;$ i4 live   & WAFT $\;896\;$ i5 & $\mathbf{0.2320}$ \\
v59 & DA3-g & 1 & yes  & WAFT $\;896\;$ i5 \emph{cached} & WAFT $\;896\;$ i5 & $0.2194$ \\
v60 & DA3-g & 2 & yes  & WAFT $\;896\;$ i5 \emph{cached} & WAFT $\;896\;$ i5 & $0.2211$ \\
v62 & DA3-g & 2 & no   & WAFT $\;896\;$ i5 \emph{cached} & WAFT $\;896\;$ i5 & $0.2228$ \\
v63 & DA3-g & 2 & yes  & WAFT $\;448\;$ i5 live       & WAFT $\;448\;$ i5 & $0.2238$ \\
\bottomrule
\end{tabular}
\end{center}

\paragraph{What the table shows once resolution is visible.} Three separate effects had been
running together, and each is isolated by holding the other columns fixed.

\emph{Cached versus live training, at one resolution and one network.} This is the augmentation
asymmetry of \S\ref{sec:waft-aug-asymmetry}, and it is worth a few thousandths:

\begin{center}\small
\begin{tabular}{lccc}
\toprule
& cached training & live training & $\Delta$ \\
\midrule
DA3-l, 2 pools, eval $448$              & v58$^{\dagger}$ $0.2507$ & v64 $0.2547$ & $+0.0040$ \\
DA3-g, 2 pools, de-flicker, eval $448$  & v60$^{\dagger}$ $0.2211$ & v63 $0.2238$ & $+0.0027$ \\
\bottomrule
\end{tabular}
\\[2pt]
{\footnotesize $^{\dagger}$Evaluated at $896$ rather than $448$. These two rows are therefore not a clean
isolation either; they bound the effect rather than measure it exactly.}
\end{center}

\emph{Evaluation resolution.} Every arm scored at $896$ sits above the comparable arm scored at
$448$, and this is the comparison that had been hidden. v64 reaches $0.2547$ against v51's
$0.2560$. However, v64 is scored from a $448$ front-end and v51 from an $896$ one, which means
v64 is not merely behind: it is behind while being handicapped in a way the earlier tables did not
show.

\emph{The evaluation front-end is not a free choice.} v50 and v51 are the \emph{same trained model},
differing only in what scored it: SEA-RAFT at $448$ gives $0.2350$, WAFT at $896$ gives $0.2560$.
That $+0.0210$ is the evaluation front-end alone, with training held fixed --- larger than any
training-side effect measured anywhere in this investigation.

\paragraph{A comparison I drew and had to withdraw.} v64 was reported here as beating v50 and hence
as showing WAFT the better front-end. That is wrong, and the table above is what makes it obvious:
v50 is scored with SEA-RAFT at $448$, the weakest evaluation in the table; beating it therefore
says nothing. The standing best is v51 at $0.2560$, and v64 is below it. The pairing was chosen because
both arms were live at one resolution, which made it look like the controlled comparison; the
control it lacked was the evaluation front-end, which the previous version of this table did not
have a column for.

\paragraph{What is actually being asked, and the arms that answer it.} The best configuration known
is SEA-RAFT-trained at $448$ and evaluated with WAFT at $896$. To displace it, a fully-WAFT arm has
to be evaluated at $896$ too --- otherwise it is competing from a weaker front-end. v65 and v66
therefore run WAFT live at $896$ on both sides, everything else held to v64 and v63 respectively:

\begin{center}\small
\begin{tabular}{lllc}
\toprule
arm & configuration & train/eval flow & target \\
\midrule
v65 & DA3-l, 2 pools               & WAFT $896$ i4 live, both sides & v51 $0.2560$ \\
v66 & DA3-g, 2 pools, de-flicker   & WAFT $896$ i4 live, both sides & v45 $0.2320$ \\
\bottomrule
\end{tabular}
\end{center}

\noindent These cost four times the pixels of the $448$ arms, whose measured training rates were
$40$ steps per minute on DA3-l and $12.5$ on DA3-g. Until they land, the honest statement is that no
fully-WAFT arm has yet been evaluated at the resolution the best result uses, and therefore that
nothing here shows WAFT beating SEA-RAFT as a front-end.

\paragraph{Half-resolution WAFT really is the weaker front-end, measured directly.} The claim that
the $448$ arms are handicapped should not rest on the metric-AJ gap it is meant to explain, and was
therefore measured on the front-end alone: WAFT's 2-D track error against ground truth, no refiner and no
depth involved, on a balanced sample of twelve minival clips. Ground truth comes from projecting
each clip's \texttt{tracks\_XYZ} with its own intrinsics, and only points the dataset marks visible
at that frame are scored.

\begin{center}\small
\begin{tabular}{lrrr}
\toprule
& \multicolumn{1}{c}{$896$ (scale $0$)} & \multicolumn{1}{c}{$448$ (scale $-1$)} & \multicolumn{1}{c}{$\Delta$} \\
\midrule
median 2-D error over clips & $3.12$\,px & $3.60$\,px & $+0.49$\,px \\
relative degradation        & ---        & $+15.7\%$  & \\
clips where $448$ is worse  & ---        & $12/12$    & \\
worst single clip           & $39.55$\,px & $57.97$\,px & $+18.42$\,px \\
\bottomrule
\end{tabular}
\end{center}

Every clip degrades, which makes this a property of the resolution rather than a few unlucky
sequences,
and the degradation is worst exactly where tracking is already hardest: the two \texttt{drivetrack}
clips with errors in the tens of pixels lose $5.60$ and $18.42$\,px, against roughly a pixel on the
well-behaved clips. An arm evaluated at $448$ is therefore fed a measurably worse 2-D track than one
evaluated at $896$, before its refiner sees anything. This is what disqualifies the v64-versus-v51
comparison, and it is independent of anything about training.

\paragraph{The refiner must be VSSD-2pool on both sides of any comparison.} Two pools is the better
refiner and is what the pipeline uses. A comparison against a one-pool arm therefore understates
the baseline rather than testing the front-end. This is satisfied on DA3-l and is not on DA3-g:

\begin{center}\small
\begin{tabular}{llcccl}
\toprule
comparison & configuration & pools match & res.\ match & baseline & status \\
\midrule
v65 vs.\ v51 & DA3-l                      & yes ($2$ vs.\ $2$) & yes ($896$) & $0.2560$ & valid \\
v66 vs.\ v45 & DA3-g $+$ de-flicker       & \textbf{no} ($2$ vs.\ $1$) & yes ($896$) & $0.2320$ & not pool-matched \\
\bottomrule
\end{tabular}
\end{center}

\noindent v45 is a one-pool arm, and no SEA-RAFT-trained DA3-g arm was ever built with two pools ---
that configuration was deliberately not run, being of no interest on its own. \textbf{v65 against
v51 is therefore the only comparison in this investigation that is matched on all four axes} --- flow network
aside: same depth backbone, same pool count, same evaluation resolution, same iteration count, live
on both sides. It is the one that decides the question. v66 measures whether full-resolution WAFT
lifts the DA3-g arm above $0.2320$, which is worth knowing, though a win there would be confounded by
the extra pool and could not be attributed to the front-end.

\subsubsection{The rebuilt table: every row self-consistent}
\label{sec:waft-rebuilt}

Every arm below trains and evaluates with the same front-end, WAFT run live at
\texttt{image\_size}~$896$, \texttt{scale}~$=-1$ and \texttt{iters}~$=4$, and every one uses
VSSD-2pool where a refiner is present. The rows they replace were trained on SEA-RAFT tracks and
scored on WAFT tracks.

\begin{center}\small
\begin{tabular}{lllrrr}
\toprule
arm & depth & configuration & paper & rebuilt & $\Delta$ \\
\midrule
v67 & DA3-l & vmamba3, one pool            & $0.246$ & $0.2485$ & $+0.0025$ \\
v64 & DA3-l & vmamba3-2pool                & $0.256$ & $\mathbf{0.2547}$ & $-0.0013$ \\
\midrule
v68 & DA3-g & vmamba3-2pool                & $0.216$ & $0.2218$ & $+0.0058$ \\
v70 & DA3-g & \quad $+$ per-frame scale head & $0.229$ & $0.2212$ & $-0.0078$ \\
v69 & DA3-g & de-flicker, standalone       & $0.203$ & $0.1998$ & $-0.0032$ \\
v63 & DA3-g & de-flicker $+$ vmamba3-2pool & $0.232$ & $0.2238$ & $-0.0082$ \\
\bottomrule
\end{tabular}
\end{center}

\paragraph{The correction was worth making and changed little.} Every row moves by less than
$0.009$, and no ordering changes within either backbone: DA3-l with two pools remains the best
configuration, and de-flicker alone remains harmful. The train/eval mismatch was therefore a real
defect in how the numbers were produced rather than a distortion of what they say --- which is the
outcome one hopes for but cannot assume, and is only knowable by rebuilding.

\paragraph{VSSD-2pool, measured with one variable changed.} v67 and v64 differ in
\texttt{two\_pool} and nothing else --- same depth backbone, same front-end, same resolution,
same iteration count, live on both sides:

\begin{center}\small
\begin{tabular}{lc}
\toprule
DA3-l refiner & mean metric-AJ \\
\midrule
one pool               & $0.2485$ \\
VSSD-2pool             & $\mathbf{0.2547}$ \\
\midrule
gain from the second pool & $+0.0062$ \\
\bottomrule
\end{tabular}
\end{center}

\paragraph{The per-frame scale head does nothing at two pools.} Against v68, which differs only in
that head, the mean moves $0.2218 \to 0.2212$, i.e.\ $-0.0007$. On a single seed that is not a
demonstrated regression; the honest reading is no measurable effect. It is consistent with the
de-flicker stage being what addresses DA3-g's per-frame scale drift, and it means the scale head
should be reported for completeness rather than as a contribution. Note that the paper's $0.229$ for
this row was a one-pool arm, so the $-0.0078$ against it is confounded by the pool change; the v68
comparison is the clean one.

\paragraph{One conclusion did change, and it is in the paper's DA3-g table.} With the DA3-g rows
rebuilt, the best DA3-g configuration ($0.2238$) now sits \emph{below} DELTA$+$DA3-g ($0.227$),
where the previous $0.232$ sat above it. The paper's claim that our DA3-g pipeline leads the
external DA3-g methods does not survive, and has been withdrawn. The claim the DA3-g table exists to
support --- that no DA3-g configuration reaches the DA3-l ceiling --- is unaffected.

\subsubsection{Four hypotheses for the DA3-g gap, and what killed each}
\label{sec:deflicker-signal}

Under a self-consistent protocol the DA3-g arm reaches $0.2238$ (v63) against $0.2320$ for v45,
which was trained on SEA-RAFT tracks and evaluated on WAFT ones. A model evaluated on the front-end
it trained with should not lose to one that was not, so the gap was treated as a defect and
searched for. It localises entirely in the de-flicker stage:

\begin{center}\small
\begin{tabular}{lccc}
\toprule
training front-end & no de-flicker & $+$ de-flicker & de-flicker's gain \\
\midrule
SEA-RAFT (v42 $\to$ v45)      & $0.2246$ & $0.2320$ & $+0.0074$ \\
WAFT cached (v62 $\to$ v59)   & $0.2228$ & $0.2194$ & $-0.0034$ \\
WAFT live (v68 $\to$ v63)     & $0.2218$ & $0.2238$ & $+0.0020$ \\
\bottomrule
\end{tabular}
\end{center}

Everything else in the pipeline matches; only what the de-flicker stage learns differs. Four
explanations were proposed and each was measured rather than argued:

\begin{center}\small
\begin{tabular}{p{5.4cm}p{6.4cm}l}
\toprule
hypothesis & measurement & verdict \\
\midrule
The two arms were scored from different-quality tracks &
Both cached track sets against ground truth, from disk: $-0.0014$ overall, medians identical at
$0.743$\,m &
dead \\
WAFT training taught a weaker correction &
The WAFT-trained de-flicker's weights are uniformly \emph{larger} ($\texttt{out.weight}$ $0.035$ vs
$0.027$) &
dead \\
WAFT's track is too clean, so the stage trains on a weaker instability signal &
Injected $2.72$\,px of AR(1) positional noise, derived to match SEA-RAFT's measured error. Result
got \emph{worse}: $0.2238 \to 0.2200$ (v71) &
dead \\
WAFT's error is less \emph{structured}, so less of it is per-frame-correctable &
Per-frame scale spread over 16 clips: SEA-RAFT $0.0294$, WAFT $0.0294$, ratio $0.998\times$ &
dead \\
\bottomrule
\end{tabular}
\end{center}

\paragraph{The noise result is worth keeping.} Adding positional noise pushed de-flicker's
contribution negative again, $+0.0020 \to -0.0018$ against the same no-de-flicker baseline. The
hypothesis predicted the opposite, so the mechanism is not that the stage needs a harder training
signal. Recorded because the noise level was derived rather than guessed --- $\sqrt{5.20^2-4.10^2}
= 3.20$\,px of displacement from the two front-ends' measured 2-D error, a per-component
$\sigma$ of $2.72$\,px, verified back at $3.25$\,px median with AR(1) correlation $+0.90$ --- and it
still failed, which makes it evidence rather than a failed guess.

\subsubsection{What the search actually found: the stage is supervised by almost nothing}
\label{sec:deflicker-supervision}

The fourth measurement returned a number that reframes the question. Alongside the per-frame scale
spread it reports the fraction of the depth error that a \emph{single per-frame scalar} can explain
--- which is precisely and only what the de-flicker can remove, since it emits one scalar per frame
and applies it to every point:

\begin{center}\small
\begin{tabular}{lcc}
\toprule
over 16 minival clips & SEA-RAFT sampling & WAFT sampling \\
\midrule
per-frame scale spread (std of log-scale over frames) & $0.0294$ & $0.0294$ \\
fraction a per-frame scalar explains                  & $0.0025$ & $0.0055$ \\
clips where that fraction exceeds $5\%$               & \multicolumn{2}{c}{$4/16$} \\
\bottomrule
\end{tabular}
\end{center}

\paragraph{Roughly $99.5\%$ of the stage's gradient is noise from its own point of view.} There is
no de-flicker-specific loss. The stage is trained only through the tracking loss,
$L = vis\cdot|xyz_{\text{pred}}-xyz_{\text{gt}}|_1/\text{scale}_{\text{gt}} + \lambda_{uv}\,vis\,
|\Delta uv|^2$, which sums per-point 3-D error over ${\sim}900$ points and $F$ frames. Of that
error, $0.3$--$0.6\%$ is the per-frame-scalar component the stage can act on; the rest is per-point
error it is architecturally incapable of changing.

That explains the observations the four hypotheses could not. If the learning signal is
overwhelmingly irrelevant, what the stage converges to is high-variance: v45's $+0.0074$ against
v63's $+0.0020$ is then variance rather than mechanism, which is why no mechanism reproduced it;
perturbing the front-end moves a noise-dominated optimisation unpredictably, in either direction;
and injecting further noise made it worse because the signal was already noise-dominated.

\paragraph{The stage also cannot see what it is meant to correct.}
Fig.~\ref{fig:deflicker-coupling} traces its inputs. DA3-g's per-frame global scale drift is a
property of the depth model --- the nested model re-fits a least-squares scalar independently each
frame --- but the stage never reads the depth map. Its four inputs are the ray (from the flow's
$uv$), the depth \emph{sampled at} that $uv$, the visibility from the flow's forward--backward
check, and a normaliser taken from those same samples. It therefore observes a property of the
depth model through a biased sample of $98$--$901$ points chosen by the tracker, which is what couples
it to the front-end at all.

\input{figs/fig_deflicker_coupling}

\paragraph{The ceiling, and a correction to what the variance figure means.} The
$0.3$--$0.6\%$ above is a share of log-error \emph{variance}, and it was briefly read here as
meaning the stage had nothing to gain. Against the metric actually reported it means no such thing.
Taking the per-frame scale from ground truth --- an oracle correction, the bound on every possible
design of this stage --- gives:

\begin{center}\small
\begin{tabular}{lcccc}
\toprule
& as-is & $+$ per-clip constant & $+$ per-frame (full oracle) & flicker alone \\
\midrule
drivetrack & $0.0078$ & $0.2455$ & $0.3492$ & $+0.1037$ \\
pstudio    & $0.2706$ & $0.5402$ & $0.5925$ & $+0.0522$ \\
adt        & $0.4667$ & $0.5121$ & $0.5683$ & $+0.0561$ \\
\midrule
ALL        & $0.2484$ & $0.4326$ & $\mathbf{0.5033}$ & $\mathbf{+0.0707}$ \\
\bottomrule
\end{tabular}
\end{center}

Variance-explained and a threshold-based metric are not interchangeable: a small systematic scale
error moves points across fixed-metre thresholds while contributing little variance. The headroom is
large, $+0.2549$ in total, and the stage delivers $+0.0020$ --- about $3\%$ of the flicker component
alone. The gradient dilution is therefore a real loss rather than a reason to abandon the stage.

\paragraph{The split says which problem is which.} Of that $+0.2549$, only $+0.0707$ is
frame-to-frame wobble; $+0.1842$ is a per-clip \emph{constant} offset, which is a calibration
problem and not flicker at all. The correction needed is dominated by that constant ($|$median$|$
p50 $0.294$) rather than the wobble around it (p50 $0.027$), and the old stage's output bound of
$0.5$ clipped $17.4\%$ of frames outright. The stage was therefore misnamed, under-ranged,
unsupervised and fed through the tracker --- four separate defects, and \S\ref{sec:depth-scale-refiner} is what
replaces it.

\subsubsection{Mamba-3 depth scale refiner}
\label{sec:depth-scale-refiner}

\texttt{Mamba3DepthScaleRefiner} replaces the de-flicker stage. The temporal model, the bounded
output and the zero-initialised head are unchanged; it remains an exact identity at
initialisation and a strict add-on to the no-refiner baseline. Four things differ, one per defect:

\begin{center}\small
\begin{tabular}{p{4.2cm}p{4.6cm}p{4.6cm}}
\toprule
defect & evidence & change \\
\midrule
Trained only through per-point 3-D error; the one scalar it emits is a few percent of that &
Delivers $+0.0020$ against a $+0.0707$ ceiling &
$L_{\text{dsr}}$, a direct target from ground-truth depth \\
Every input arrives through the flow &
Couples it to the front-end; biased ${\sim}900$-point sample &
Reads the depth map \\
\texttt{max\_scale\_correction} $=0.5$ &
Clips $17.4\%$ of frames; needed $|\Delta s|$ reaches $2.44$ &
$2.5$, which clips none \\
Named for frame-to-frame wobble &
The constant term is $10\times$ larger at the median &
Renamed; the per-frame scale is unconstrained \\
\bottomrule
\end{tabular}
\end{center}

\paragraph{Algorithm.} Per frame the depth map is taken in log space, pooled to $64\times64$,
normalised by \emph{fixed} dataset constants and encoded by a small convolutional stack into one
token. The token sequence goes through the same time-axis Mamba-3 as before, and a zero-initialised
head emits $\Delta s_f = 2.5\tanh(\cdot)$, applied as $z = z_{\text{raw}}\exp(\Delta s_f)$ along the
frozen ray.

The normalisation constants are fixed rather than per-clip deliberately: subtracting a per-clip
median would remove exactly the absolute-scale cue that predicting the constant offset depends on,
and that offset is ten elevenths of the available gain.

\paragraph{Loss.}
\[
  \Delta s^{*}_f = \mathrm{median}_{n \in \text{visible}}\!\left[\log z_{\text{gt}}(f,n) - \log
  z_{\text{raw}}(f,n)\right], \qquad
  L_{\text{dsr}} = \frac{\sum_f w_f\,|\Delta s_f - \Delta s^{*}_f|}{\sum_f w_f},
\]
added to the tracking loss as $\lambda_{\text{dsr}} L_{\text{dsr}}$. The median resists the handful
of badly tracked points whose log-ratios are orders of magnitude out. The weight $w_f =
\sqrt{n_{\text{visible}}(f)}$ follows the standard error of a median; an earlier version required
eight visible points per frame and silently discarded every clip with fewer than eight
\emph{tracks} in total, which was a third of batches and the small clips rather than the occluded
ones.

$\lambda_{\text{dsr}} = 1$ is a starting value, not a derived one: $L_{\text{dsr}}$ runs $0.05$ to
$0.24$ on typical batches against a tracking loss of $0.01$ to $0.66$, which puts the two at the
same order.
It is the first thing to sweep if the arm disappoints.

\subsubsection{Explanations that were proposed and eliminated}
\label{sec:waft-eliminated}

Recorded because each cost real time, and because the pattern --- a plausible mechanism supported by
two measurements, then refuted by a third --- recurred often enough to be worth warning about.

\begin{center}
\begin{tabular}{lll}
\toprule
proposed cause & test & result \\
\midrule
evaluation tracks differ    & v45 re-scored on the new tracks & $0.2320\to0.2320$ \\
2-D input quality           & both branches, identical batches & WAFT better, $0.69$ vs $0.80$\,px \\
arms not comparable         & config diff, code equivalences   & no mechanism; $\sim$$5\times10^{-6}$ \\
visibility gate erases input& GT visibility at inference       & closes part, not the gap \\
depth distribution differs  & $z$ sampled at each uv           & identical; $z_{\mathrm{ref}}$ ratio $0.9998$ \\
systematic scale bias       & end-to-end $\log(z/z^{*})$       & both $\approx1.018\times$ \\
temporal flicker            & error variance across frames     & WAFT smoother, $0.79\times$ \\
de-flicker actively harmful & v62, stage removed               & $+0.0017$ only \\
\bottomrule
\end{tabular}
\end{center}

\noindent The flicker test deserves its own note, since it was the closest structural analogy
available: if WAFT were better per frame but noisier across frames, a per-track refiner mixing along
time could not see or repair that, exactly as \S\ref{sec:v44} describes for DA3-g's depth scale.
Measured on tracks visible through a whole $24$-frame window, WAFT's 2-D error is not only smaller
but moves less between frames --- flicker $0.79\times$ and frame-to-frame jerk $0.74\times$
SEA-RAFT's, against a ground-truth motion of $3.21$\,px per frame. The analogy does not hold. One
gap remains in that test: it uses only never-occluded tracks, so flicker localised at occlusion
boundaries, where the two front-ends disagree most, would not appear in it.

\subsection{The depth-scale-refiner line (v72--v90)}
\label{sec:dsr-line}

Every arm here attacks one number: on DA3-g our best self-consistent 2-pool arm, v63, scores
$0.2238$ absolute metric-AJ against DELTA's $0.2274$. The deficit is $0.0036$, and it is not spread
across the benchmark.

\subsubsection{The gap is adt, and only adt}
\label{sec:dsr-where}

\begin{center}\small
\begin{tabular}{lrrr}

subset & v63 (ours) & DELTA & gap \\
\midrule

pstudio    & $0.2027$ & $0.1936$ & $\mathbf{+0.0091}$ \\
adt        & $0.3056$ & $0.3244$ & $\mathbf{-0.0188}$ \\
\midrule
mean       & $0.2238$ & $0.2274$ & $-0.0036$ \\
\bottomrule
\end{tabular}
\end{center}

We already beat DELTA on pstudio and tie on drivetrack. Recovering $56\%$ of the adt gap would flip
the mean. The oracle decomposition (\texttt{result/adt\_error.log}, APD3D after an oracle fix, so
each column is a ceiling) says where the headroom is:

\begin{center}\small
\begin{tabular}{lrrrrr}
\toprule
subset & as-is & $+$bias & $+$scale & $+$GT ray & vis.\ agree \\
\midrule
drivetrack & $0.0078$ & $0.2455$ & $0.3492$ & $0.3953$ & $0.7828$ \\
pstudio    & $0.2706$ & $0.5402$ & $0.5925$ & $0.6458$ & $0.6949$ \\
adt        & $0.4667$ & $0.5121$ & $0.5683$ & $0.6124$ & $0.7849$ \\
\bottomrule
\end{tabular}
\end{center}

v63 reaches $0.4837$ APD3D on adt against a per-frame-scale ceiling of $0.5683$, so $0.0846$ APD3D
is unclaimed --- roughly $0.053$ metric-AJ on adt at the $\approx 0.63\times$ conversion, or
$0.018$ on the mean. The gap to DELTA is $0.0036$. Capturing a fifth of what we already leave on
adt would be enough, which is what motivated a scale estimator that reads the whole depth map
(\S\ref{sec:depth-scale-refiner}) rather than only the tracked points.
