Nothing in this line beats v63. Most of it cannot be interpreted at all, for four separate reasons.

\subsubsection{Defect 1: a DA3-l-trained stage fed DA3-g depth}
\label{sec:dsr-contam}

Every composite from v73 to v85 loads \texttt{model.scale\_init} pointing at a scale refiner trained
by \texttt{train\_scale\_standalone.py}, which read \texttt{DA3\_ROOT} --- hardcoded to DA3-l. The
tracker itself reads DA3-g. \texttt{doc/plan\_da3g\_reeval.md} had already excluded exactly this for
the DA3-l-trained refiners v33/v35/v39, on the grounds that feeding them DA3-g zero-shot ``only
shows the over-correction collapse''. It was reintroduced anyway, because nothing enforced it.

Only v63, v72 and the v88 warm start are free of it.

\subsubsection{Defect 2: the scale refiner was trained against another dataset's error}
\label{sec:dsr-target}

\texttt{build\_cache} computed its regression target as
$r=\log z_{\mathrm{gt}}-\log z_{\mathrm{pr}}$, where $z_{\mathrm{pr}}$ is the \emph{WAFT
prediction's} own $z$. That prediction set was unprojected with DA3-l depth, while the model's input
is the DA3-g map. The refiner therefore learned to read a DA3-g map and emit the DA3-l correction.
DA3-l under-estimates drivetrack depth ($\approx 0.55\times$) so its correction is positive; DA3-g
over-estimates and needs a negative one. Measured on minival, against its own target
$ds^{*}=\mathrm{median}_n[\log z_{\mathrm{gt}}-\log z_{\mathrm{raw}}]$:

\begin{center}\small
\begin{tabular}{lrrrrr}
\toprule
subset & $|ds^{*}|$ before & after & change & true $ds^{*}$ & predicted \\
\midrule
drivetrack & $0.0879$ & $0.5725$ & $+551.3\%$ & $+0.014$ & $+0.586$ \\
pstudio    & $0.2393$ & $0.2536$ & $+6.0\%$   & $+0.231$ & $+0.250$ \\
adt        & $0.0726$ & $0.0676$ & $-6.8\%$   & $+0.035$ & $+0.009$ \\
\bottomrule
\end{tabular}
\end{center}

The failure is a gross \emph{over-correction}, not an inverted sign: on drivetrack the stage emits
$+0.586$ where the truth is $+0.014$, roughly forty times too large, because $+0.586$ is the
correction DA3-\emph{l} needs. It therefore worsens the very quantity it exists to fix.
This explains the whole downstream pattern at once: v89$^{-}$ (the stage alone) scores $0.1435$
where applying no correction scores $0.208$; substituting it for the de-flicker peaks at $0.2142$;
and when the same stage is added behind a learnable gate the optimiser drives that gate
\emph{negative} ($-0.0054$ after $500$ steps), an attempt to shrink a signal that is too large.

Retraining against the correct target exposes the deeper point. The error to be fixed is half what
the buggy target implied --- emitting zero costs $0.1632$ on DA3-g against $0.3260$ on the DA3-l
residual --- and of that, the model recovers only $2.3\%$, against $73.3\%$ before. The per-frame
scale error on DA3-g is both small and largely \emph{unpredictable from the depth map}. The premise
of the module holds on DA3-l, whose $\approx 0.55\times$ far-field bias is a large systematic
signal, and very nearly fails on DA3-g.

Two lessons. A module's own training objective is not evidence that it helps: the $73.3\%$ was
honest and meaningless. And a diagnostic is only as good as its calibration --- an earlier version
of this measurement used invented intrinsics ($f_x=f_y=500$, principal point at the image centre)
and reported a true $ds^{*}$ of $-0.927$ on drivetrack, which made the defect look like a sign
inversion. With the clip's real \texttt{fx\_fy\_cx\_cy} the truth is $+0.014$.
\texttt{scripts/check\_scale\_on\_minival.py} now uses the recorded intrinsics and reports signed
means alongside the error, so both the magnitude and the direction are visible.

\subsubsection{Defect 3: the validation signal measured nothing}
\label{sec:dsr-val}

Two independent faults, both in the monitoring path:

\begin{itemize}
\item \texttt{val\_clips} was sampled \emph{from} \texttt{train\_clips} and \texttt{train\_clips} was
never reduced, so the ``VAL'' loss was a training loss on training clips.
\item \texttt{TAPVid3DDataset} redraws a random temporal window and a random query subset on every
\texttt{\_\_getitem\_\_}; \texttt{augment=False} disables only photometric augmentation. Consecutive
validation passes therefore measured different data.
\end{itemize}

The second dominates. v82, at $\mathrm{lr}=3\mathrm{e}{-}6$ where the weights barely move, produced
validation values spanning $0.49$ to $1.14$ --- a $2.3\times$ noise floor at an essentially fixed
model. Every curve comparison in v73--v82 was smaller than that. Both are fixed
(\texttt{data.split.holdout\_val}, and the dataset RNG re-seeded per validation pass), and
\texttt{train.val\_at\_step0} now records each run's own starting point.

With a correct signal the loss behaves: v84 went from validation $0.5615$ / metric-AJ $0.0843$ at
step $0$ to $0.1197$ / $0.2097$ at step $1000$ --- both moving together, which is what undertraining
looks like and what the broken signal had made impossible to see.

\subsubsection{Defect 4: three settings that silently did nothing}
\label{sec:dsr-noop}

\begin{itemize}
\item \texttt{train.init\_ckpt} was read only from a \texttt{--init-ckpt} CLI flag. v75 set it in
YAML, trained without it, and silently became a duplicate of v76 --- their validation matches to
four decimals. The run's \texttt{cfg.json} recorded the warm start that never happened.
\item \texttt{model.freeze\_scale} lived inside the \texttt{scale\_init} branch, so an arm
warm-started through \texttt{init\_ckpt} was never frozen while the log implied it was.
\item The best-checkpoint save was gated on \texttt{early\_stop\_patience > 0}, and
\texttt{\_save\_ckpt} prunes all but the newest checkpoint, so the best point on a curve was
unrecoverable. v78a's step-$750$ weights, its best, were lost this way.
\end{itemize}

All three are now read from the config and applied however the weights arrived, and the best
checkpoint is saved unconditionally.

\subsubsection{Substituting the de-flicker, and adding it}
\label{sec:dsr-swap}

The de-flicker and the depth-scale refiner are duplicates: both \texttt{per\_frame\_logscale}
methods return one bounded $(B,F,1)$ log-scale per frame, the first from the tracked points, the
second from the whole depth map. Two stages estimating the same quantity is not a pipeline, so the
scale refiner replaces the de-flicker.

Measured with the buggy stage 1, substitution is expensive: untrained it costs pstudio
$0.2027\!\to\!0.0642$ and adt $0.3056\!\to\!0.1322$. Freezing stage 1 and re-adapting v35 recovers
pstudio entirely ($0.2017$) but only $77\%$ of adt, and \emph{loses} drivetrack
($0.1590\!\to\!0.1447$); unfreezing reaches $0.2142$, still short of v63.

As a control, \texttt{Mamba3V88} \emph{adds} the stage to v63 behind a zero-initialised gate, so
$ds=\mathrm{gate}\cdot ds_{\mathrm{scale}}+ds_{\mathrm{deflicker}}$ and the model is numerically
identical to \texttt{Mamba3V45} at initialisation --- verified directly,
$\max|xyz_{V45}-xyz_{V88}|=0$. Its warm start scores $0.2238$, reproducing v63 to four decimals and
confirming both the identity and that the iters-4/5 protocol difference is below $0.001$.

\subsubsection{v89 and v90: the line rerun on a corrected stage 1}
\label{sec:dsr-v8990}

With defect 2 fixed --- the target is now the residual against DA3-g depth sampled at the WAFT $uv$,
and the cache key carries a \texttt{TARGET\_VERSION} so the old targets cannot be reused --- the
two arms that matter are rebuilt and renumbered, because a corrected stage 1 makes them different
models rather than reruns:

\begin{center}\small
\begin{tabular}{ll}
\toprule
arm & configuration \\
\midrule
v89   & the corrected DA3-g scale refiner \emph{alone} (stage 2 zero-init, i.e.\ the identity) \\
v90a  & WAFT $+$ (DA3-g $+$ v89's refiner) $+$ vmamba3-2pool, stage 1 frozen \\
v90b  & the same, both stages joint, continued from v90a's best \\
\bottomrule
\end{tabular}
\end{center}

v89 isolates what the depth-scale stage contributes by itself, against two references on the same
protocol: no correction at all is $0.208$, and the same arm with the \emph{buggy} stage 1 scored
$0.1435$ --- worse than doing nothing, which is what exposed the over-correction. The corrected
stage 1 clears the gate at $2/3$ subsets improved (pstudio $-4.7\%$, adt $-9.4\%$, drivetrack
$+4.1\%$), and \textbf{v89 scores $0.2100$} (drivetrack $0.1496$, pstudio $0.1807$, adt $0.2996$)
--- recovering $+0.0665$ over v89$^{-}$ and landing marginally above the $0.208$ that no correction
gives. Its predictions are nearly constant at $+0.016$ and it removes only $2.3\%$ of the DA3-g
scale error, so this is a correct module measuring a genuinely small effect, not a broken one. That
also bounds what v90 can gain from stage 1: the de-flicker and v35 together add $+0.016$ over no
correction, of which stage 1 here contributes about $+0.002$. v90 is the swap:
stage 1 replaces the de-flicker, stage 2 is warm-started from v63's v35.

Two gates run before any training, because the previous attempt failed silently in exactly these
two places. First, stage 1 must reduce the minival scale error on at least two of three subsets ---
its own held-out objective is explicitly not accepted as evidence, having improved $73.3\%$ while
inverting the sign. Second, the untrained v90 warm start must beat $0.15$, the buggy arm having
scored $0.1184$ there. Either gate failing stops the run rather than spending hours on a stage that
worsens the quantity it exists to fix.

\subsubsection{One registry for the depth choice}
\label{sec:dsr-registry}

The DA3-l/DA3-g choice was made four ways --- a \texttt{DA3\_ROOT} env var, a
\texttt{--da3-depth-root} flag, a \texttt{data.da3\_depth\_root} key, and hardcoded paths --- with
the loader copy-pasted into six scripts, fourteen scripts defaulting to DA3-l silently, and no
derived artifact recording which depth produced it. That is what allowed defects 1 and 2, and what
made both invisible.

The choice now lives in one module, \texttt{mamba3\_tracker/data/depth\_source.py}: one
registry, one loader; \texttt{resolve()} identifies a raw path instead of trusting it, \texttt{stamp()} writes provenance
beside an artifact and \texttt{verify()} refuses a mismatch. Verification is deliberately
use-dependent --- a track's $uv$ is invariant to scaling along the ray (verified,
$\max|\Delta uv| = 2.3\mathrm{e}{-}13$), so a DA3-l track set is a valid $uv$ source for a DA3-g
run, while its $z$ is not. All seven existing WAFT caches are retro-stamped \texttt{da3l}.



\subsection{Where the adt gap to DELTA actually is (v91)}
\label{sec:adt-gap}
adt is the subset to attack. It is where the DA3-g deficit lives ($-0.0182$ metric-AJ against
DELTA$+$DA3-g), and on pstudio we are already ahead by $+0.0159$. Splitting adt's 50 clips into the
two things metric-AJ multiplies together --- how close the predicted point is, and whether it is
called visible --- shows they point in opposite directions.

\begin{figure*}[!tb]
\centering
\includegraphics[width=\textwidth]{figs/adt_gap.png}
\caption{\textbf{adt, 50 clips, v91 against DELTA$+$DA3-g, paired per clip.} Points above the
diagonal are clips where we are better (below, for the error panel). Our 3D positions are the
closer ones on two thirds of clips --- points within threshold on $33/50$, median error on $32/50$
--- and metric-AJ is still worse on $34/50$, because occlusion accuracy is worse on $41/50$. The
deficit is entirely in the visibility channel.}
\label{fig:adt-gap}
\end{figure*}

\begin{table}[h]
\centering
\caption{adt, mean over 50 clips. Every position measure favours us; the headline does not.}
\label{tab:adt-split}
\begin{tabular}{lccc}
\toprule
 & DELTA$+$DA3-g & v91 & \\
\midrule
points within threshold      & 0.4688 & \textbf{0.4831} & we win, 33/50 clips \\
median 3D error (m)          & 0.2406 & \textbf{0.2037} & we win, 32/50 clips \\
mean 3D error (m)            & 0.6844 & \textbf{0.3911} & we win by a wide margin \\

\paragraph{The visibility we are scored on was never predicted.}
\texttt{eval\_metric3d.py} returns the visibility it was handed, which is the optical-flow
front-end's forward--backward consistency mask. It is not learned and not refined; the refiner's own
\texttt{vis\_logits} were a zero placeholder that nothing consumed. DELTA predicts visibility.

\paragraph{What the channel is worth.}
Scoring the \emph{same} predicted positions against ground-truth visibility
(\texttt{-{}-oracle-vis}; a ceiling, not a result) gives the following. Points-within-threshold is
unchanged to four decimals, so the positions really are identical and the whole difference is the
visibility channel.

\begin{table}[h]
\centering
\caption{adt with the visibility channel replaced by an oracle. The gap to DELTA is $0.0196$; the
channel is worth $+0.1146$, six times that.}
\label{tab:adt-oracle}
\begin{tabular}{lccc}
\toprule
adt & metric-AJ & pts within thresh & occlusion acc. \\
\midrule
DELTA$+$DA3-g               & 0.3244 & 0.4688 & 0.8257 \\
v91, flow visibility        & 0.3048 & 0.4831 & 0.7846 \\
v91, \emph{oracle} visibility & \textbf{0.4194} & 0.4829 & 1.0000 \\
\bottomrule
\end{tabular}

\subsubsection{Why the visibility head could not be trained as the pipeline stands}
\label{sec:vis-head-blocked}
Adding a zero-initialised \texttt{vis\_head} to the v35 refiner, freezing everything else, and
supervising it with a BCE against ground-truth visibility is a four-tensor change and was
implemented (v92). It cannot learn, for a reason that turned out to matter far more than the head:

\begin{quote}
\textbf{The training batches do not contain the supervision the configuration asks for.} With
\texttt{num\_tracks: 256} and an $8$-frame window, the median number of tracks actually supervised
per item is \textbf{8} on pstudio, \textbf{8} on adt and \textbf{35} on drivetrack, with a minimum
of \textbf{3}.
\end{quote}

The cause is in \texttt{TAPVid3DDataset.\_\_getitem\_\_}: after a random $8$-frame window is cropped
from a $300$-frame clip, only queries whose \emph{anchor frame} falls inside that window are kept.
Roughly $8/300$ of them do. Every optimiser step is therefore supervised by about $8\times8 = 64$
point-observations rather than the $256\times8 = 2048$ the config specifies --- about $3\%$.

This is the most plausible explanation yet for the three findings that preceded it, and it sits
upstream of all of them:
\begin{itemize}
  \item the per-clip loss CV of $2.7$ --- a mean over $\sim$8 points is bound to
        be violently noisy;
  \item v91d's training-loss slope of $-0.00025\pm0.00257$ per 100 steps over 4244 clips, i.e.\ no
        measurable learning at any rate from $5\times10^{-6}$ to $5\times10^{-4}$;
  \item and directly for the head at hand, $38$--$62\%$ of items have \emph{every} point visible,
        so the visibility BCE starts at $-\log(1-\epsilon)\approx0.001$ with no negatives to learn
        from. That is exactly what v92 printed at step 0.
\end{itemize}

The fix is a data change, not a model one: re-anchor every query to the sampled window (the branch
that already runs when \emph{no} anchor falls inside it), or sample the window around a query's
anchor. It changes what every previous number in this report means, so it is recorded here rather
than applied.


\subsection{The visibility head (v92)}
\label{sec:vis-head}
Every metric-AJ number in this report is scored with a visibility that was never predicted.
\texttt{eval\_metric3d} returns the visibility it was handed, which is the optical-flow
front-end's forward--backward consistency mask; the refiner emitted a zero placeholder that
nothing consumed. DELTA predicts visibility, and on adt that difference is the whole deficit
(\S\ref{sec:adt-gap}): our points are the closer ones, and only the occlusion term separates us.

\paragraph{The module.} \texttt{v35.vis\_head} is a two-layer MLP, $128\to64\to1$, $8321$
parameters, applied to the same per-point feature $x$ the depth and $\Delta uv$ heads read. It
emits a logit per point per frame, and $\sigma(\cdot)$ of that is the predicted visibility.
Figure~\ref{fig:vis-head} places it beside the mask it was meant to replace.

\paragraph{It predicts outright, not as a residual.} The tempting form is a correction to the
flow mask's logit, and it is unusable: the mask must be clamped away from $0$ and $1$ to keep the
logit finite, which puts the base at $\pm6.9$, so a head that must travel $6.9$ to flip one point
moves about $0.06$ in a short fine-tune. Such a head is a no-op by construction and reports
\emph{identical} metrics with it on and off --- which is exactly what the first attempt did.
Predicting the logit directly removes that ceiling; at initialisation the logits span
$[-0.31,+0.26]$, so either decision is reachable from step~0. It is consequently \emph{not}
zero-initialised: identity at step~0 is neither achievable nor needed, because
\texttt{-{}-vis-source flow} supplies the untouched mask whenever the comparison calls for it.

\paragraph{The head does not lack the mask's evidence.} The trunk's per-point input is
$[\,\mathbf{r},\,z/z_{\text{ref}},\,v\,]$ plus the depth patch and the DINOv3 feature, so the flow
mask $v$ is already its fourth channel. The head sees that mask \emph{and} appearance, depth and
temporal context. That it still loses to the mask alone on adt is therefore a statement about the
trunk's features, not about missing information.

\paragraph{Supervision.} A binary cross-entropy against ground-truth visibility, weighted by
\texttt{loss.lambda\_vis} and applied \emph{on top of} the normalised loss weights rather than
inside them: \texttt{loss.weights} is renormalised to sum to one, so adding a term there would
silently change \texttt{pos\_3D}'s share and confound the depth path. This follows
\texttt{lambda\_dsr}.

\paragraph{Switching it on and off costs nothing.} With everything but the head frozen, one
checkpoint serves both arms: \texttt{-{}-vis-source flow} ignores the head, \texttt{model} uses
it, and the predicted 3-D positions are bit-identical between them. The comparison therefore
isolates the visibility channel exactly, with no second training run.

\paragraph{Outcome.} Four attempts, all negative; the measurements are in \S\ref{sec:v93}. The
head loses to the flow mask on adt under every class weighting tried, and
\texttt{-{}-vis-source flow} remains the default. The $+0.1146$ oracle headroom on adt is real and
unclaimed.

\input{figs/fig_vis_head}

\subsection{The DA3-g arm that reaches DELTA, and what produced it (v93/v93b)}
\label{sec:v93}
This is where the DA3-g line stops. \textbf{v93b matches DELTA$+$DA3-g on absolute metric-AJ}
($0.22744$ against $0.22737$), and the entire gain comes from one repair to the training data
sampler --- not from the optimiser, the loss, or the architecture, all of which were tried and
excluded first.

\subsubsection{Protocol}
\paragraph{Architecture.} WAFT $+$ (DA3-g $+$ Mamba-3 depth scale refiner) $+$ vmamba3-2pool,
the \texttt{v73} composite. Stage~2 carries \emph{three} heads on the shared per-point trunk
feature: \texttt{dz\_head} for depth along the ray, \texttt{duv\_head} for the bounded 2-D
correction, and \texttt{vis\_head} for visibility (\S\ref{sec:vis-head}). All three are trained
here; only the first two affect the reported metric, because the evaluator is run with
\texttt{-{}-vis-source flow} and takes visibility from the front-end's forward--backward mask.
\texttt{vis\_head} is therefore present and supervised but inert at scoring time, which is what
makes the head's on/off comparison exact.

Two runs forming one trajectory: v93b resumes v93 from its last checkpoint with the optimiser and
scheduler state intact, so the pair is a single $8297$-step run rather than two fine-tunes.

\begin{table}[h]
\centering
\caption{v93/v93b. Everything but the sampler matches v91, so the sampler is the only difference
from the arms that preceded it.}
\label{tab:v93-protocol}
\begin{tabular}{ll}
\toprule
initialisation & \texttt{result/v73fix\_warmstart/ckpt\_0.pt}, whose \emph{v35} is bit-identical
                 to v63's \texttt{ckpt\_20000} \\
learning rate & $5\times10^{-5}$, no warmup, cosine over the last $20\%$ of steps \\
batch / window & $1$ / $8$ frames \\
\textbf{sampler} & \texttt{data.reanchor\_window: true} \\
steps & $3191$ (v93) $+$ $5106$ (v93b) $=$ $8297$ \\
wall clock & $\approx$3.7\,s/step, about 8.5\,h in total on one 12\,GB GPU \\
\bottomrule
\end{tabular}
\end{table}

\begin{table}[h]
\centering
\caption{Every component, and whether it trains. The frozen mass is what makes the arm cheap: of

arm & mean & drivetrack & pstudio & adt \\
\midrule

\textbf{v93b} \texttt{ckpt\_8297} & \textbf{0.22744} & 0.16309 & \textbf{0.20918} & 0.31004 \\

\paragraph{Report it as a tie, not a win.} The margin over DELTA is $+0.00007$, three orders of
magnitude inside the $0.0056$ seed-to-seed spread measured from v63's two seeds ($0.2238$ vs
$0.2182$). On one seed each the two are indistinguishable. The number that does survive is the
\emph{paired} gain over its own initialisation, $+0.00858$, which carries no seed noise because
both sides are the same run.

\subsubsection{What produced it: the sampler, and nothing else}
\label{sec:v93-sampler}
\texttt{TAPVid3DDataset} cropped a random $8$-frame window from a $\sim$300-frame clip and then
kept only the queries whose \emph{anchor frame} fell inside it. Anchors are spread across the
whole clip, so $8$ frames catch about $2.7\%$ of them: after \texttt{max\_queries} had already cut
$824$ to $256$, a \textbf{median of 8 tracks} survived per item on adt and pstudio, minimum 3.
Every optimiser step was supervised by roughly $64$ point-observations instead of $2048$.

The anchor marks which frame is the \emph{question}, not a different kind of annotation --- every
point is labelled in every frame, and measurably the anchor is the point's first visible frame
only $1$--$3\%$ of the time and frame 0 only $0.4\%$, while the point is visible at its anchor
$100\%$ of the time. A point anchored outside the window is therefore a valid query inside it,
asked from a frame where it is visible, and its query pixel is its own 3-D position projected
there --- which reproduces the released \texttt{queries\_xyt} to under a pixel for $97$--$99\%$ of
points. With \texttt{reanchor\_window} the median rises from $9$ to $121$ tracks on adt and $6$ to
$114$ on pstudio.

This is a \emph{training-data} change only. \texttt{eval\_metric3d} loads whole clips with every
query and no anchor filter, so no measured number --- ours or any baseline's --- is affected by
it. Configs that produced an already-published number pin \texttt{reanchor\_window: false} in
their own file so they still reproduce.

\subsubsection{Three levers that did nothing, on this same arm}
Each was measured before the sampler was suspected, and each is a negative result worth keeping:

\begin{itemize}
  \item \textbf{Learning rate.} An overfit ladder at $5\times10^{-6}$, $5\times10^{-5}$,
        $1.5\times10^{-4}$ and $5\times10^{-4}$, then v91d overnight at the best of them with
        4-clip gradient accumulation: training-loss slope $-0.00025\pm0.00257$ per 100 steps
        ($0.1\sigma$) over $4244$ clips, validation $0.1877\to0.1884$, metric-AJ
        $0.2235\to0.2231$. The run was stationary, not diverging, so no smaller rate helps either.
  \item \textbf{Gradient noise.} \texttt{batch>1} is unavailable --- clips carry depth maps of
        different resolutions (drivetrack $280\times504$, adt $504\times504$) and collate stacks
        them --- so accumulation was used instead, giving the same $\sqrt{4}$ reduction. No effect.
  \item \textbf{A learned visibility head} (\S\ref{sec:vis-head-blocked}), four attempts, closed
        below.
\end{itemize}

\subsubsection{Neither loss curve is usable here: 7 of 7}
\label{sec:v93-curves}
Figure~\ref{fig:v93-curve} is the point of this section as much as the result is. Across all
$8297$ steps the training loss sits flat at $\approx$0.2 with no trend, and the held-out
validation wanders $0.131$--$0.138$ and never falls below its own step-0 value --- while metric-AJ
rises $+0.0086$. Validation's own pick, \texttt{best/ckpt\_5500}, scores $0.2231$ against
\texttt{ckpt\_8297}'s $0.2274$: it selected the worst of the three scored checkpoints.

Counting every occasion in this line, \textbf{validation has mispredicted the direction of
metric-AJ seven times out of seven}. Two nights of this work were spent concluding ``it is not
learning'' from curves that could not have shown learning --- at \texttt{batch: 1} the per-clip
loss spans $21\times$, and the logger was printing a single clip's loss per 50 steps. The
practical rule for this pipeline is to score checkpoints and not to read curves.

\begin{figure*}[!tb]
\centering
\includegraphics[width=\textwidth]{figs/v93_curve.png}
\caption{\textbf{v93 and v93b as one trajectory.} \emph{Top:} training loss, flat throughout.
\emph{Middle:} held-out validation, also flat, with its minimum at step $5500$ --- the checkpoint
that scored worst. \emph{Bottom:} metric-AJ on the four scored checkpoints, the only signal that
moved, against DELTA's $0.22737$. The fitted trend is $+0.00090\pm0.00038$ per 1000 steps
($2.4\sigma$), but it rests on four non-monotonic points and the final leg contains the
end-of-schedule learning-rate anneal, which a longer run gets only once.}
\label{fig:v93-curve}
\end{figure*}

\subsubsection{Whether more steps would go further}
The trend on the scored checkpoints is $+0.00090\pm0.00038$ per 1000 steps, which extrapolates to
$\approx$0.232 at 14k steps. Three things argue against taking that at face value: only four
points and non-monotonic (step $5500$ fell $0.0022$ below step $3191$); the last leg mixes real
progress with the cosine anneal from $5\times10^{-5}$ to $5\times10^{-6}$ over steps
$6638$--$8297$, which is not repeatable per 1000 steps; and at $+0.0009$ per 1000 steps it takes
$\sim$6000 steps to move by one seed spread. Settling it needs a longer run with the anneal placed
at the end of the longer horizon, scored every 2000 steps, before repeat seeds are worth spending.

\subsubsection{The visibility head, closed after four attempts}
adt is where the remaining deficit to DELTA lives, and \S\ref{sec:adt-gap} showed it is entirely
the visibility channel: our 3-D positions are the closer ones, and scoring them against
ground-truth visibility gives $0.4194$ on adt against $0.3048$, so the channel is worth $+0.1146$
where the gap to DELTA is $0.0196$. Four attempts failed to claim any of it.

\begin{enumerate}
  \item \textbf{A residual on the flow mask's logit.} Unusable by construction: the mask must be
        clamped away from 0 and 1 to keep the logit finite, putting the base at $\pm6.9$, and the
        head moved $\pm0.057$ in 400 steps. No point crossed the $0.5$ threshold and adt scored
        $0.2995/0.4766/0.7849$ identically with the head on and off.
  \item \textbf{Direct prediction, 8-frame windows.} Trained where $94.6\%$ of observations are
        visible and evaluated where $39$--$75\%$ are. Per-subset damage tracked that gap exactly:
        pstudio $+0.0238$ (closest to the training rate), drivetrack $-0.0081$, adt $-0.0653$,
        with adt's occlusion accuracy collapsing $0.7849\to0.5482$.
  \item \textbf{Whole-clip features, cached.} The trunk is frozen, so its per-point feature is a
        constant of the data: caching it (280 clips, 3\,M observations per subset, 2.0\,GB) turns
        the fit into an MLP over a fixed array and removes the distribution mismatch. The head
        still lost to the flow mask on adt, $0.6051$ against $0.7666$, predicting ``visible''
        $81\%$ of the time where the truth is $57\%$.
  \item \textbf{Class weighting}, the last untried knob, swept against the cached features:
\end{enumerate}

\begin{table}[h]
\centering
\caption{Held-out visibility accuracy against the flow mask. No setting beats it on adt, the
subset the work is aimed at.}
\label{tab:vis-posweight}
\begin{tabular}{lccc}
\toprule
 & adt & drivetrack & pstudio \\
\midrule
flow mask (the bar) & \textbf{0.7666} & 0.7790 & \textbf{0.7551} \\
\midrule
\texttt{pos\_weight} 0.3 & 0.5942 & 0.6627 & 0.6651 \\
\texttt{pos\_weight} 0.5 & 0.6176 & 0.7619 & 0.7592 \\
\texttt{pos\_weight} 1.0 & 0.6051 & 0.7971 & 0.7477 \\

The trunk features do not carry enough occlusion evidence for a small head to beat a
forward--backward flow check, and \texttt{-{}-vis-source flow} remains the default. The $+0.1146$
headroom on adt is real and unclaimed.


\subsection{Visibility from flow alone (v94)}
\label{sec:v94}

Every number up to and including v93b is scored with the front-end's forward--backward
consistency mask. v92 (\S\ref{sec:vis-head}) tried to learn a replacement from the refiner's
trunk feature and failed. v94 is the same objective attacked from the other side: a head whose
only input is the optical flow itself, independent of the depth refiner by construction
(Fig.~\ref{fig:v94-head}).

\subsubsection{Why v92 could not work}
Two defects, and the second is the one that makes a flow-only head the right shape.

\textbf{v92 could satisfy its loss by copying its own input.} The trunk's per-point token is
$[\mathbf{r},\,z/z_{\text{ref}},\,v]$ plus the depth patch and the DINOv3 feature, and $v$ is
the forward--backward mask. A head reading that token therefore had the answer it was being
trained to reproduce available as one of its inputs, and echoing it is a near-zero-loss
solution. The measurement is consistent with exactly that: head on and head off returned
$0.2995$, $0.4766$ and $0.7849$ for adt metric-AJ, point accuracy and occlusion accuracy --
identical to four decimals on all three.

\textbf{The mask cannot express what the ground truth contains.} In
\texttt{searaft\_flow.flow\_tracker}, one cycle residual per hop is compared against a fixed
tolerance, and the result is \emph{latched} along each sweep:
\begin{equation}
v_{t+1} = v_t \wedge \big(\lVert d_t + b_t\rVert \le \alpha(\lVert d_t\rVert + \lVert b_t\rVert) + \beta\big).
\label{eq:latch}
\end{equation}
By \eqref{eq:latch}, $v_t$ is monotone non-increasing: a point marked invisible once can never
be marked visible again, at any threshold. Ground truth does not behave that way
(Table~\ref{tab:reappear}).

\begin{table}[h]
\centering
\caption{Points whose visibility returns after an occlusion, on the $150$ minival clips. The mask
cannot produce a single one, by construction; the majority of ground-truth points are one.
\emph{visible} is the mean fraction of (point, frame) pairs marked visible.}
\label{tab:reappear}
\begin{tabular}{lrrrrr}
\toprule
 & points & \multicolumn{2}{c}{re-appearing} & \multicolumn{2}{c}{visible} \\
\cmidrule(lr){3-4}\cmidrule(lr){5-6}
subset & & ground truth & mask & ground truth & mask \\
\midrule
adt        & $34\,851$ & $21\,952$ \ ($63.0\%$) & $0$ \ ($0.0\%$) & $0.572$ & $0.521$ \\
drivetrack & $12\,800$ & $6\,694$ \ ($52.3\%$)  & $0$ \ ($0.0\%$) & $0.793$ & $0.731$ \\
pstudio    & $14\,041$ & $9\,206$ \ ($65.6\%$)  & $0$ \ ($0.0\%$) & $0.751$ & $0.543$ \\
\bottomrule
\end{tabular}
\end{table}

This is the mechanism behind adt's oracle headroom of $+0.1146$ (\S\ref{sec:adt-gap}): scoring the
same 3-D positions against ground-truth visibility raises adt from $0.3048$ to $0.4194$, and a
mask that can only ever turn points off is structurally unable to recover most of that.

\subsubsection{Architecture}
Per point per frame the head reads two vectors --- $f_t$, the flow from $t$ to $t+1$ sampled at
the point's tracked position, and $b_t$, the flow from $t$ to $t-1$ at the same place --- and
forms eight scalars from them: $f_t/32$, $b_t/32$, $\log(1+\lVert f_t\rVert)$,
$\log(1+\lVert b_t\rVert)$, and the two cycle residuals $\log(1+\lVert f_t+b_{t+1}\rVert)$ and
$\log(1+\lVert f_{t-1}+b_t\rVert)$. The residuals are the quantity \eqref{eq:latch} thresholds;
they are supplied explicitly because a product of two inputs is awkward for an MLP, and they add
no information the two vectors do not already carry. An MLP embeds the eight to $64$, two
Mamba-3 layers mix \emph{along frames only} --- never across points --- and a linear head with a
sigmoid gives $P(\text{visible})$ per frame. There is no latch. The head is $0.173$\,M parameters.

\paragraph{Two switches, which are independent and easily confused.}
The first is whether the head is used at all: the evaluator's \texttt{-{}-vis-source} selects
\texttt{flow}, the forward--backward mask, or \texttt{v94}, this head. Every arm before v94 is
\texttt{flow}. The second is internal to the head and fixed at training time, \texttt{model.bidirectional},
which sets the decay mask of the Mamba-3 layers over the frame axis:
\begin{align}
\text{causal:} \quad & L[i,j] = \gamma_j \textstyle\prod_{k=j+1}^{T} \alpha_k, \label{eq:v94-causal}\\
\text{non-causal:} \quad & L[i,j] = \gamma_j \big[\textstyle\prod_{k=j+1}^{T} \alpha_k + \prod_{k=1}^{j-1} \alpha_k\big].
\label{eq:v94-noncausal}
\end{align}
Under \eqref{eq:v94-causal} frame $t$ sees only frames $\le t$; under \eqref{eq:v94-noncausal} it
sees the whole clip. \textbf{Both settings are the head switched on} --- they are two separately
trained copies of one architecture whose configurations differ in that single line, and both are
reported below. Non-causal ought to be the more accurate of the two, because an occlusion is
bracketed in time and a point is known to have been occluded partly by the fact that it returns,
which is evidence lying in future frames. It costs the streaming property that v93b's causal
refiner keeps, which is why the causal variant is measured rather than assumed away.

\input{figs/fig_v94_head}

It sees no depth, no appearance and no refiner state, so it trains standalone in minutes rather
than inside the refiner's $8.5$\,h run, and retraining the refiner cannot invalidate it.

\subsubsection{Protocol}
The flow is cached with v93's exact front-end settings, and the cache is accepted only if the
mask it recomputes is identical to the WAFT predictions v93b was scored against --- measured
over all $150$ minival clips, $0$ clips disagreed. Training and held-out clips are disjoint from
minival.

\subsubsection{Result}
\label{sec:v94-result}
The head was scored on both depth backbones and both metrics (Table~\ref{tab:v94-result}). Its
effect replicates almost exactly across the two, which is what should happen to a module that
never sees depth: the DA3-l arm is a different refiner trained against a different depth model,
and the head moves it the same way and by nearly the same amount.

\begin{table}[h]
\centering
\caption{v94 against the mask it replaces, on both depth backbones and both metrics. Within each
block the two rows share one checkpoint and therefore have \emph{identical} 3-D positions, so the
comparison is paired and the difference is the visibility channel alone. The DA3-l arm is
\mbox{WAFT+DA3-l+vmamba3-2pool} (v64) and the DA3-g arm
\mbox{WAFT+(DA3-g+Mamba-3 depth scale refiner)+vmamba3-2pool} (v93b); DA3-l carries no scale
refiner, because that stage exists to correct DA3-g's per-frame drift. The DA3-l control reproduced v64's recorded
$0.2547 / 0.1207$ exactly.}
\label{tab:v94-result}
\begin{tabular}{llrrrrr}
\toprule
metric & visibility & drivetrack & pstudio & adt & mean & vs.\ mask \\
\midrule
\multirow{4}{*}{absolute metric-AJ}
 & DA3-g, mask & $0.1631$ & $0.2092$ & $0.3100$ & $0.2274$ & --- \\
 & DA3-g, v94  & $0.1539$ & $0.2336$ & $0.2986$ & $0.2287$ & $+0.0013$ \\
 & DA3-l, mask & $0.1410$ & $0.2852$ & $\mathbf{0.3379}$ & $0.2547$ & --- \\
 & DA3-l, v94  & $0.1324$ & $\mathbf{0.3139}$ & $0.3226$ & $\mathbf{0.2563}$ & $+0.0016$ \\
\midrule
\multirow{4}{*}{normalised 3D-AJ}
 & DA3-g, mask & $0.1113$ & $0.0332$ & $0.1281$ & $0.0909$ & --- \\
 & DA3-g, v94  & $0.0910$ & $0.0305$ & $0.1077$ & $0.0764$ & $-0.0145$ \\
 & DA3-l, mask & $0.1096$ & $0.0959$ & $\mathbf{0.1565}$ & $\mathbf{0.1207}$ & --- \\
 & DA3-l, v94  & $0.0899$ & $0.0825$ & $0.1332$ & $0.1019$ & $-0.0188$ \\
\bottomrule
\end{tabular}
\end{table}

\textbf{The head helps the absolute metric negligibly and costs the leaderboard metric a great
deal.} On absolute metric-AJ it gains $+0.0013$ on DA3-g and $+0.0016$ on DA3-l; on normalised
3D-AJ it loses $-0.0145$ and $-0.0188$, and there it loses on \emph{every} subset of both
backbones --- including the pstudio that gains most on the absolute metric. The two metrics
disagree because the normalised one median-scales each prediction before thresholding, which
makes its thresholds relative and much tighter: mean AJ is $0.09$--$0.12$ rather than
$0.23$--$0.25$, so the extra points the head marks visible are tested far more severely.
\textbf{v94 is therefore not adopted as the default}, and \texttt{-{}-vis-source flow} remains
what every headline number in this report is scored with. The two metrics sit on different
scales, so the raw differences understate how lopsided the exchange is; as fractions of the score
being traded, on DA3-l it is $+0.6\%$ absolute against $-15.6\%$ normalised, a loss about
$25\times$ the gain.

\textbf{The gain is also the fragile side of that exchange.} Broken out by subset on DA3-l, the
absolute metric improves on \emph{one} subset and degrades on two --- pstudio $+10.1\%$,
drivetrack $-6.1\%$, adt $-4.5\%$ --- so the $+0.0016$ mean is one gain narrowly outrunning two
losses, and a small movement in any of the three would flip its sign. The normalised metric shows
no such cancellation: drivetrack $-18.0\%$, pstudio $-14.0\%$, adt $-14.9\%$, every subset in the
same direction and by a similar amount, on both backbones. A consistent loss across six subset
comparisons is a measurement; a near-cancellation on one of them is not a result to build on. The
honest description is that the head is good on pstudio and bad on drivetrack and adt, and that
one metric's arithmetic happens to reward that mixture by a hair while the other penalises it
heavily.

\textbf{Held-out visibility accuracy did not predict metric-AJ, in either direction.} This is the
result worth carrying forward. On drivetrack the head is the more accurate classifier by
$+0.0382$ (Table~\ref{tab:v94-gate}) and yet costs $-0.0092$ absolute AJ on DA3-g and $-0.0086$
on DA3-l; and the causal head, which won the accuracy gate ($+0.0072$ against $+0.0055$),
\emph{lost} the AJ comparison ($0.2266$ against $0.2287$). AJ credits a point only where
visibility and position are \emph{both} right, so marking more points visible exposes more of
them to the position test, and a classifier that is better in isolation can still lower the
score. Visibility accuracy is not a usable proxy for either metric here, and an ablation
reporting only the former is not evidence.

\textbf{Where it works, it works well, and it replicates.} pstudio gains $+0.0244$ on DA3-g and
$+0.0287$ on DA3-l in absolute metric-AJ --- the largest single-subset movement from one channel
anywhere in this report, twice over. pstudio is also where the mask is worst calibrated: it marks
$0.543$ of points visible against a ground truth of $0.751$ (Table~\ref{tab:reappear}), so it has
the most to give back.

\textbf{adt was not recovered} on either backbone, as \S\ref{sec:v94} anticipated: $-0.0114$ on
DA3-g and $-0.0153$ on DA3-l. The $+0.1146$ oracle headroom there remains unclaimed after five
attempts --- four in v92 and this one.

\textbf{What may and may not be concluded.} Within each block the comparison is paired --- same
weights, same positions, deterministic evaluation --- so the differences are exact and carry no
run-to-run noise. They are nonetheless one training draw of the head, whose seed sensitivity is
unmeasured. Against DELTA$+$DA3-g ($0.22737$) the comparison is \emph{unpaired}, so the $0.0056$
seed spread of \S\ref{sec:v93} applies and $0.2287$ remains a tie, not a win. That the same
effect appears on two independently trained refiners over two depth models, with the same sign on
all six subset comparisons of the normalised metric, is stronger evidence than either arm alone.

\subsection{Training setup, common to every arm (all versions)}
Only the $0.40$M-parameter refiner is trained. The loss is 3D-position-only (the 2D track and
visibility are frozen, so they carry no gradient):
\begin{equation}
\mathcal{L} = \frac{1}{|\mathcal{V}|}\sum_{(t,n)\in\mathcal{V}}
\frac{\lVert \hat{\mathbf X}_{t,n}-\mathbf X^{*}_{t,n}\rVert_1}{s_{\text{clip}}},
\end{equation}
an $\ell_1$ error over visible points, normalised by the per-clip median anchor-frame depth
$s_{\text{clip}}$ (matching the benchmark's scale convention). We train $20$k steps (AdamW,
lr $3\!\times\!10^{-4}$, warmup $500$, $8$-frame windows, bf16; $\sim$9.5\,h on one GPU).


% =====================================================================
\section{Final result}
\label{sec:final}

This chapter is the single place to look for what the work achieved. Every number in it was
produced by our own evaluation pipeline on the same hardware, on the $150$-clip TAPVid-3D
minival split, and every one is traceable to a surviving evaluation directory. Arms whose
numbers are known to be produced by a defect are \emph{not} reported here; they are listed in
\S\ref{sec:final-excluded} with the defect named, and their measurements remain in the
methodology chapter where the defect is diagnosed.

\subsection{Headline}
\label{sec:final-headline}

\begin{table}[h]
\centering
\caption{The result, in four numbers. Each cell is the best arm that is free of the defects of
\S\ref{sec:final-excluded}, beside the strongest external method under the identical protocol.
Three decimals throughout, as in every other table here. Neither DA3-g absolute entry is bold: the
two agree at this precision, and at full precision their difference is $79\times$ smaller than the
run-to-run spread, so they are indistinguishable.
The two metrics disagree by design, and that disagreement is the finding: fixed-metre thresholds
reward getting the depth \emph{scale} right, median scaling removes scale from the comparison
before it is scored.}
\label{tab:final-headline}
\begin{tabular}{llrlr}
\toprule
depth & \multicolumn{2}{c}{ours (best defect-free arm)} & \multicolumn{2}{c}{best external} \\
\cmidrule(lr){2-3}\cmidrule(lr){4-5}
 & arm & score & method & score \\
\midrule
\multicolumn{5}{l}{\emph{absolute metric-AJ} (fixed metre thresholds, $1$\,cm--$2.56$\,m)} \\
DA3-l & v64, WAFT$+$DA3-l$+$2pool & $\mathbf{0.255}$ & DELTA$+$DA3-l & $0.183$ \\
DA3-g & v93b, WAFT$+$DA3-g$+$scale ref.$+$2pool & $0.227$ & DELTA$+$DA3-g & $0.227$ \\
\midrule
\multicolumn{5}{l}{\emph{normalised 3D-AJ} (median-scaled, the leaderboard metric)} \\
DA3-l & v64, WAFT$+$DA3-l$+$2pool & $0.121$ & DELTA$+$DA3-l & $\mathbf{0.141}$ \\
DA3-g & v93b, WAFT$+$DA3-g$+$scale ref.$+$2pool & $0.091$ & DELTA$+$DA3-g & $\mathbf{0.109}$ \\
\bottomrule
\end{tabular}
\end{table}

\textbf{What we claim.} On \emph{absolute} metric-AJ the refiner leads by a wide margin on DA3-l
--- $0.255$ against DELTA's $0.183$, a $39\%$ relative gain --- and matches DELTA on DA3-g
(both $0.227$). The DA3-g match is a \emph{tie}, not a win: at full precision v93b is nominally the
higher of the two, by $+0.00007$, which is $79\times$ smaller than the $0.0056$ seed-to-seed
spread measured from two seeds of v63 (\S\ref{sec:v93}). A margin that far inside the noise of a
single arm is not an ordering, and we do not claim one.

\textbf{What we do not claim.} On the scale-invariant leaderboard metric we \emph{lose} to DELTA
on both backbones, $0.121$ against $0.141$ and $0.091$ against $0.109$. This is not a
reporting choice but a property of the method: the refiner corrects depth along the ray, and
median scaling divides exactly that correction out before scoring. The reversal is examined in
\S\ref{sec:abs}.

\textbf{The single best absolute number measured in this work} is $0.256$, from v64 scored with
the v94 flow-only visibility head (\S\ref{sec:v94-result}). It is recorded rather than adopted,
because the same substitution costs $15.6\%$ of the normalised metric to buy $0.6\%$ here; v64 at
$0.255$ is the configuration the work treats as its result.

\subsection{What is excluded from this chapter, and why}
\label{sec:final-excluded}

Three defects produced numbers that were at one time reported. Each is diagnosed in the
methodology chapter; none of the affected numbers appears in the tables below.

\begin{table}[h]
\centering
\caption{Measurements excluded from this chapter. ``Inflated'' means the defect raised the score,
which is why none of them was caught by looking at the numbers alone.}
\label{tab:final-excluded}
\begin{tabular}{llll}
\toprule
arm & reported & defect & effect \\
\midrule
v39 & $0.246$ & trained on SEA-RAFT, scored on WAFT (\S\ref{sec:searaft-trained-waft-eval}) & inflated \\
v51 & $0.256$ & trained on SEA-RAFT, scored on WAFT & inflated \\
v45 & $0.232$ & trained on SEA-RAFT, scored on WAFT & inflated \\
\midrule
v51 & $0.253$ & scored on $18914$ of $25814$ frames after adt clips hit & understated \\
v50 & $0.222$ & out-of-memory on the quadratic path (\S\ref{sec:collapse-reeval}) & understated \\
\midrule
v35 & $0.234$ & not reproducible from any surviving evaluation (\S\ref{sec:collapse-reeval}) & --- \\
\midrule
v74 & $0.179$ & never loaded the checkpoint it was meant to test (\S\ref{sec:dsr-arms}) & invalid \\
v81 & $0.173$ & training data contaminated & invalid \\
\bottomrule
\end{tabular}
\end{table}

\textbf{The train/eval mismatch is the one that matters}, because it affected the two arms that
had been reported as the overall winners. The refiner sees flow during training ---
\texttt{track\_clip} runs inside the training loop --- so an arm trained on SEA-RAFT and scored
on WAFT is handed a better front-end than it was fitted to. The gain is real but cannot be
credited to the refiner, and the matched pair proves the size of it: v50 and v51 are the
\emph{same trained weights}, and scoring them with SEA-RAFT gives $0.235$ where WAFT gives
$0.256$. The arms reported below hold the front-end fixed at WAFT for training and evaluation
alike, which is the only configuration that answers how good the pipeline is on WAFT.

\subsection{The scale-invariant leaderboard metric}
Table~\ref{tab:median} reports median-scaled 3D-AJ.
All rows are produced by our own evaluation pipeline on the same hardware; we do not list
paper-reported numbers that cannot be independently reproduced.
The strongest result among the externally-published methods is \emph{DELTA}~\cite{delta}
(DenseTrack3D, ICLR~2025): mean 3D-AJ $0.141$ (drivetrack / pstudio / adt $=0.130$ / $0.141$ / $0.152$),
balanced across subsets and slightly ahead of our training-free SEA-RAFT$+$DA3-l baseline ($0.117$).
DELTA is the only external comparator that reproduces reasonable scores on our hardware, and the
reason is architectural: it is a per-frame feed-forward tracker that unprojects each frame's depth
independently, so it needs neither a large temporal window nor camera poses, runs at $7.7$\,fps
(full minival in ${\sim}1$\,h) and fits comfortably in 12\,GB.
Our learned v33 \emph{loses} on this scale-invariant metric (it trades relative-structure quality
for absolute-scale correctness; see \S\ref{sec:abs}).

The remaining external methods underperform for reasons specific to this 12\,GB GPU, \emph{not} an
evaluation-harness artefact. SpatialTrackerV2\,$^{*}$ must run with $s_{\text{wind}}{=}60$ (the paper
uses $s_{\text{wind}}{=}500$, requiring ${\sim}40$\,GB; see \S\ref{sec:spatrackerv2}), which caps its
accuracy. TAPIP3D$+$DA3-l collapses on the rotation-heavy egocentric subsets (adt $0.004$, pstudio
$0.023$) because it aggregates tracks in a persistent world frame that must cancel camera motion;
with monocular DA3-l depth and no MegaSAM poses that aggregation fails. Crucially, TAPIP3D's \emph{own}
official evaluator reproduces these same numbers on \texttt{da3\_minival} to three decimals, confirming
the limitation is a genuine property of pose-free TAPIP3D$+$DA3-l rather than a scoring bug. (On the
different, larger full-eval split TAPIP3D~\cite{tapip3d} scores ${\sim}0.30$; the full six-method
minival comparison is in \S\ref{sec:sota}.)
TrackCraft3R~\cite{trackcraft3r} (arXiv~2026) reports Sim(3)-aligned AJ of $0.73$ (pstudio) and
$0.86$ (adt) in the paper; under our standard protocol (all 150 clips, 50 per subset) it scores
$0.013$ / $0.010$ / $0.003$ on pstudio / adt / drivetrack.
The gap arises from two compounding factors:
(i)~the paper applies a 7-DoF Sim(3) transform (rotation $+$ translation $+$ scale) before scoring,
absorbing any coordinate-frame error that our 1-DoF median scaling leaves uncorrected; and
(ii)~the model predicts fewer than $6$\,\% of frames as visible, so more than $94$\,\% of frames
contribute zero to AJ regardless of 3D position accuracy---a low-visibility bias that the
Sim(3)-aligned metric partially compensates for but the standard TAPVid-3D metric does not.
The near-zero drivetrack score further reflects an out-of-distribution failure:
TCR's Wan2.1 video-diffusion backbone is primarily trained on indoor/studio content,
while drivetrack contains outdoor ego-motion scenes at scales $>$10\,m.


\subsection{Absolute metric-AJ, and the reversal}
\label{sec:abs}
Under the absolute-metric protocol the ranking \emph{reverses} (Table~\ref{tab:abs},
Fig.~\ref{fig:reversal}).

\paragraph{The best absolute metric-AJ, and why it is not the default.}
The highest absolute metric-AJ in this report is $0.256$, from
\mbox{WAFT+DA3-l+vmamba3-2pool} (v64) scored with the v94 flow-only visibility head
(\S\ref{sec:v94-result}). It is recorded for completeness rather than adopted: the same
substitution costs $-15.6\%$ on the normalised leaderboard metric to buy $+0.6\%$ here, so the
configuration this report treats as its best is the same arm with the front-end's
forward--backward mask, \textbf{v64 at $0.255$}. Both are well ahead of the older v39
(WAFT flow $+$ the v35 depth refiner; \S\ref{sec:waft}) at $0.246$, which topped this metric
before the two-pool refiner and is the arm the comparisons in the rest of this section are
written against; the SEA-RAFT-fed v35 follows at $0.225$. Taking v35 vs.\ the DA3-l baseline: metric-AJ $+53\%$ ($0.147\!\to\!0.225$),
mean error $-55\%$ ($4.29\!\to\!1.91$\,m). Against TAPIP3D$+$DA3-l: metric-AJ
$+125\%$ ($0.100\!\to\!0.225$). The gain is most concentrated where DA3-l's scale is most
wrong --- far-field \emph{drivetrack}, metric-AJ $0.005\!\to\!0.130$ ($26\times$) ---
while near-field \emph{adt} sees moderate improvement. Swapping the SEA-RAFT front-end for
WAFT (v39) lifts every subset again (drivetrack $0.130\!\to\!0.141$, pstudio $0.274\!\to\!0.279$,
adt $0.298\!\to\!0.318$).
The leaderboard's median scaling hides all of this: v35/v39's normalised 3D-AJ is
\emph{lower} than SEA-RAFT$+$MegaSAM (Table~\ref{tab:median}).


\subsubsection*{Why BootsTAPIR + ZoeDepth cannot be evaluated under the absolute metric}
\label{sec:abs-na}
ZoeDepth~\cite{zoedepth} is a \emph{relative} depth estimator: it predicts depth up to an
unknown per-video scale factor. The standard TAPVid-3D leaderboard compensates by applying a
per-clip median ratio $s^{*} = \mathrm{median}(\hat{d} / d_{\mathrm{gt}})$ before scoring
(\emph{median scaling}). Under our absolute protocol this rescaling is deliberately removed ---
fixed-metre thresholds ($1\,\text{cm}\ldots 2.56\,\text{m}$) are applied directly to the raw
predicted depths. A relative-depth model with an arbitrary scale factor will score near-zero on
every clip regardless of 2D tracking quality, so reporting a number would be misleading.
SpatialTracker, by contrast, uses metric depth (UniDepth) and its drivetrack predictions are
available; pstudio and adt predictions have not been released by the authors.

\begin{figure}[h]
\centering
\includegraphics[width=\textwidth]{figs/fig_metric_reversal.png}
\caption{Median scaling hides metric-depth quality. Left: the scale-invariant leaderboard 3D-AJ
favours SEA-RAFT$+$MegaSAM. Right: under the absolute metric, v35 wins --- most on far-field drivetrack.}
\label{fig:reversal}
\end{figure}

\subsection{Comparison to the state of the art}
\label{sec:sota}

\paragraph{SpatialTracker.}
SpatialTracker's official predictions are released only for \emph{drivetrack}.
Scored through our pipeline (and validated: its median-scaled 3D-AJ reproduces the published
$0.058$), v33 beats it in absolute metric: metric-AJ $0.082$ vs $0.069$ ($+20\%$),
metric-APD3D $0.134$ vs $0.106$ ($+26\%$).

\paragraph{SpatialTrackerV2.}
\label{sec:spatrackerv2}
SpatialTrackerV2~\cite{spatrackerv2} reports $24.7\%$ mean normalised 3D-AJ on the TAPVid-3D
minival.  We evaluated SpatialTrackerV2 on the \emph{same} RTX\,4080\,Laptop\,(12\,GB) GPU used
for our own method to ensure a hardware-fair comparison.
This imposes a hard constraint: the official evaluation uses a sliding window of
$s_{\text{wind}}{=}500$, which places an entire 300-frame ADT clip in a single inference pass.
Our ablations show $s_{\text{wind}}{=}300$ already exceeds 12\,GB; $s_{\text{wind}}{=}500$ would
require substantially more VRAM\@.  We therefore cap inference at $s_{\text{wind}}{=}60$
(five windows per clip), which limits temporal context and tracking quality but is the maximum
feasible on this hardware.  The $s_{\text{wind}}$ constraint is the dominant source of the gap
between our reproduced scores and the paper's 24.7\%: controlled ablations (varying only
$s_{\text{wind}}$ on the same clips) confirm that fixing the secondary output-format bug
(using the globally-consistent \texttt{track2d\_pred} output with per-frame reprojection
instead of the window-local \texttt{track3d\_pred}) recovers only ${\sim}0.2$--$0.9$ percentage
points, while the remaining ${\sim}24$ pp gap is attributable to the window-length difference.
Our evaluation of SpatialTrackerV2 is therefore a \emph{same-hardware, same-constraints} baseline:
the reported scores reflect what SpatialTrackerV2 achieves within the 12\,GB budget, on the
same clips as our own method.  Our best arm (v64, WAFT flow $+$ the VSSD-2pool refiner) achieves
$15.7\%$ normalised 3D-AJ on adt under these identical conditions, exceeding our SpaTrackerV2
reproduction on that subset by a wide margin.

\paragraph{TAPIP3D + DA3-l.}
Using the same DA3-l depth as our pipeline, TAPIP3D~\cite{tapip3d} scores higher than v33 on the
normalised leaderboard metric (drivetrack $0.065$ vs $0.057$, pstudio $0.023$ vs $0.041$), but
\emph{lower} on the absolute metric: mean metric-AJ $0.100$ vs $0.180$ ($-44\%$).
This reversal arises because TAPIP3D's point-trajectory model optimises for relative
pixel-track coherence, whereas our depth refiner directly corrects metric scale.

\paragraph{TAPIP3D + MegaSAM and why it fails on our minival.}
\label{sec:megasam-fail}
TAPIP3D's published SOTA ($\sim$0.30 normalised AJ) uses MegaSAM (DAv1 $+$ UniDepth $+$ DROID-SLAM $+$ CVD)
run on the \emph{original source videos at full frame-rate}.
Our minival is distributed as NPZ clips that sub-sample and trim the originals; we cannot recover the
source video, so we run MegaSAM on the NPZ frames directly.
DROID-SLAM, which is the pose-estimation backbone inside MegaSAM, requires dense, continuously
moving video ($\geq$10\,fps, sufficient translational parallax) to reliably triangulate metric scale.
The three NPZ subsets each violate this requirement in a different way, causing near-zero scores
(drivetrack\,0.00\%, pstudio\,0.27\%, adt\,0.36\%) regardless of the normalised metric's scale-invariance:

\begin{itemize}
\item \textbf{drivetrack}: the 10\,fps Waymo clips are sub-sampled to $\approx$2.6\,fps in the NPZ
  (66 frames over 25\,s). DROID-SLAM's optical-flow front-end degrades on such sparse inputs,
  producing depths of $\sim$0.9\,m where the true scene is $\sim$15\,m ($17\times$ scale error).
  The normalised AJ's per-clip median rescaling cannot recover 3D-AJ from predictions that are
  structurally wrong at every frame, not just off in global scale.

\item \textbf{pstudio}: Panoptic Studio cameras are fixed to a stationary dome; there is essentially
  no translational ego-motion. DROID-SLAM cannot triangulate metric scale without parallax, so it
  collapses depth to $\approx$1\,m (true depth 2--4\,m, 2--4$\times$ error). Normalised AJ
  partially recovers (0.27\%); absolute metric-AJ is 0\%.

\item \textbf{adt}: 300-frame clips exceed available VRAM in DROID-SLAM's global bundle adjustment
  ($>$11.6\,GB at 12\,GB). Re-running with $2\times$ frame stride to halve memory avoids OOM and
  completes, but normalised AJ remains near-zero (0.36\%) and absolute metric-AJ is 0\%.
  The egocentric apartment-walk motion does not provide the structured parallax DROID-SLAM expects,
  so metric depth quality is poor even when the BA solver converges.
\end{itemize}

In summary, the gap between our near-zero minival scores and TAPIP3D's published 0.30 is an
\emph{evaluation-condition mismatch}, not a failure of TAPIP3D itself.
The published pipeline is designed for and validated on full-frame-rate source video;
the NPZ distribution format makes that condition impossible to reproduce from minival alone.

\paragraph{TrackCraft3R.}
\label{sec:trackcraft3r}
TrackCraft3R~\cite{trackcraft3r} (arXiv~2026) repurposes the Wan2.1-T2V-1.3B video diffusion
transformer for dense 3D tracking via single-step regression.
We evaluate it on all 150 minival clips using our standard pipeline (windowed inference,
12 frames per window, query-centric windows normalised to frame-0 camera space).
Results are shown in Tables~\ref{tab:median} and~\ref{tab:abs}.

On the leaderboard (scale-invariant) metric, TCR scores $1.3\%$ / $1.0\%$ / $0.3\%$
on pstudio / adt / drivetrack (mean $0.9\%$) --- below all our baselines.
On the absolute metric, TCR scores $2.5\%$ / $3.2\%$ / $0.2\%$ (mean $2.0\%$).
Our best arm v64 outperforms TCR on absolute metric-AJ by nearly $13\times$ overall
($25.5\%$ vs $2.0\%$) and by $70\times$ on drivetrack ($14.1\%$ vs $0.2\%$) where TCR
effectively fails.

The low drivetrack score is an out-of-distribution failure: Wan2.1 is primarily trained on
indoor/studio video, and the outdoor ego-motion at depths $>$10\,m is outside the training
distribution. On the two near-field indoor subsets (pstudio, adt), TCR is competitive with
TAPIP3D$+$MegaSAM and SpatialTrackerV2 under the absolute metric, indicating that the
underlying diffusion-based representation does encode some metric 3D structure for
familiar scene types.
Absolute-metric pstudio and adt performance ($2.5\%$, $3.2\%$) remains far below our
method ($27.4\%$, $29.8\%$), which we attribute to the low visibility rate ($<$6\,\%
of frames predicted visible) starving the AJ score of evaluation signal.

As noted in \S\ref{sec:abs}, TCR is also roughly $200\times$ slower than our pipeline
(Table~\ref{tab:efficiency}), confirming that diffusion-based tracking carries a
substantial inference-time cost relative to our lightweight SSM refiner.

\paragraph{DELTA --- why it is strong on adt (and only there).}
DELTA~\cite{delta} is the strongest external method on the DA3-g backbone, but its win is concentrated
almost entirely on \textbf{adt}: absolute metric-AJ $0.324$ (best of all methods), versus a middling $0.164$
on drivetrack and $0.194$ on pstudio, where our pipeline beats it. Why so good on adt in particular? In plain
terms: \emph{DELTA is a dense tracker that reasons over many points and many frames at once, and adt is the
one benchmark where that actually pays off.} Three things line up on adt:
\begin{itemize}
  \item \textbf{Near and clear.} adt is an indoor apartment at $0.5$--$5$\,m with rich texture, so
  correspondence and depth are reliable --- a solid substrate for dense tracking. (drivetrack is
  $30$--$100$\,m, where depth is unreliable.)
  \item \textbf{The scene moves together.} adt is filmed from head-worn Aria glasses, so most tracked points
  are the \emph{static} room; their apparent motion is just the camera's own movement --- one smooth, shared
  motion for the whole frame. A tracker that pools information across all points and frames can lock onto
  that single coherent motion and get every point right at once.
  \item \textbf{No independent chaos.} pstudio, by contrast, is full of people moving \emph{independently}
  (fast, articulated, occluding), so there is no shared motion to exploit; drivetrack adds fast forward
  driving and far depth. In both, DELTA's many-point/many-frame advantage largely evaporates.
\end{itemize}
So it is \emph{not} ``multi-frame tracking'' by itself that helps --- if it were, DELTA would lead
everywhere. It is multi-frame tracking on a scene whose motion is \emph{coherent} (near-field, static room,
shared ego-motion), which only adt provides. This also explains why our depth/scale work (v42--v45) cannot
close the adt gap: our refiner corrects each point's depth in isolation and never sees the shared camera
motion. Closing it needs a model that estimates that shared motion across all points --- exactly the
\S\ref{sec:v46} proposal.

\paragraph{DELTAv2 (DenseTrack3Dv2).}
DELTAv2~\cite{deltav2} is the direct successor to DELTA --- reported ${\approx}5\times$ faster at comparable
accuracy --- and is \emph{lighter} ($51.4$\,M trainable vs DELTA's $59.2$\,M). We evaluated it identically
(fed the same DA3-l and DA3-g depth caches; $0$ failures on all $150$ clips). It reproduces DELTA's profile
almost exactly: the same adt stronghold and the same far-field drivetrack limit. On \textbf{DA3-l} it in fact
sets the single best \emph{adt} score of \emph{any} method (absolute metric-AJ $0.345$, a hair over DELTA's
$0.344$), with mean $0.180$ ($\approx$ DELTA $0.183$); on \textbf{DA3-g} it is a touch behind DELTA
(adt $0.319$ vs $0.324$, mean $0.216$ vs $0.227$). So the DELTA family --- v1 and v2 --- owns adt but nothing
else: our v64 (DA3-l, mean $0.255$) and v93b (DA3-g, mean $0.227$) still lead the \emph{mean} absolute metric,
and beat both on drivetrack and pstudio. On throughput, DELTAv2 and DELTA are comparable in our $12$\,GB
external-depth harness (${\approx}6.9$ vs $7.7$\,fps); the paper's $5\times$ speedup is measured on native
full-resolution \emph{dense} tracking, whereas our sparse-query, precomputed-depth setup is memory-bound and
does not exercise that regime.

Each of the four result tables above is followed immediately by its own bar chart
(Figs.~\ref{fig:6method-norm}, \ref{fig:6method-norm-da3g}, \ref{fig:6method-abs}
and~\ref{fig:6method-abs-da3g}), so the numbers and their visual comparison are read together
rather than pages apart.

The DA3-g pair (Figs.~\ref{fig:6method-norm-da3g} and~\ref{fig:6method-abs-da3g}) repeats the
comparison over the methods that consume DA3-g (external SOTA plus v40/v41/v42). On the
normalized metric the picture is unchanged from DA3-l (median rescaling hides the scale fix); on absolute
metric-AJ every direct-unprojection method leaps on drivetrack, but no DA3-g pipeline --- ours included ---
overtakes the DA3-l v39 on the near-field subsets (cf.\ \S\ref{sec:v41}).


\subsection{Qualitative}
Figures~\ref{fig:qual-pstudio}--\ref{fig:qual-adt} compare \textbf{v39} (WAFT flow $+$ the
vmamba3 depth refiner) against the closest similar-size state of the art,
\textbf{DELTA$+$DA3-l}, on the clip where \emph{v39} scores its highest per-clip absolute metric-AJ in each
subset --- i.e.\ our method at its best in 3D space. Both methods share the same DA3-l metric-depth backbone,
so the comparison isolates the tracker; DELTA runs its own DenseTrack3D 2D tracker (it cannot consume our WAFT
front-end). \emph{These panels were rendered from v39, whose \textbf{score} is excluded from this chapter for the
train/eval mismatch of \S\ref{sec:final-excluded}.} They are kept because what they show --- the shape of the
refiner's depth correction against the ground truth --- is a property of the refiner and not of which front-end
trained it; regenerating them from v64 is outstanding, and no number is read off them.
Solid $=$ prediction, dashed $=$ ground truth. Both panels of a figure use the \emph{same} axis
limits, taken from the ground-truth extent, so predictions that fall outside the true scene volume simply do
not appear (matplotlib line-clipping to the box). \textbf{The message is that v39 beats the SOTA in absolute
3D:} its solid tracks sit on the dashed ground truth, whereas DELTA's drift off it. To make the win checkable
rather than asserted, in every panel the \textcolor{red}{red circle} marks the track where v39 reduces the 3D
error over DELTA the most --- the identical physical region is circled in the DELTA and the v39 panel, so the
two are directly comparable. The contrast is starkest on drivetrack (Fig.~\ref{fig:qual-drivetrack}), the
far-field driving scene where DELTA collapses depth so badly that its predictions leave the ground-truth
volume entirely, while v39 keeps the tracks on the true metric depth.

\paragraph{Model size under DA3-g (parameter-matched comparison).}
Table~\ref{tab:efficiency} counts parameters for the \textbf{DA3-l} pipeline ($0.35$\,B depth backbone).
Our strongest \emph{absolute}-metric results, however, use the larger \textbf{DA3-g} backbone
(\texttt{DA3Nested-Giant-Large}, $1.40$\,B; v40--v46). Table~\ref{tab:efficiency-g} reports the parameter
budget in that setting, so the reader can see how large the models really are and that the DA3-g comparison
is \emph{parameter-matched} to the strongest external trackers rather than won with an outsized model.
Swapping DA3-l$\to$DA3-g raises our \emph{total} (frozen flow $+$ depth $+$ DINOv3 $+$ trainable refiner)
footprint from ${\approx}0.44$\,B (v39) to ${\approx}1.49$\,B (v42). Crucially, TAPIP3D and DELTA run on the
\emph{very same} DA3-g depth, so their totals rise identically --- TAPIP3D$+$DA3-g ${\approx}1.43$\,B,
DELTA$+$DA3-g ${\approx}1.46$\,B --- and SpatialTrackerV2 ($1.23$\,B, own depth) and TrackCraft3R ($1.3$\,B)
sit in the same band. The comparison is therefore genuinely \emph{parameter-matched} (everyone ${\approx}1.2$--$1.5$\,B),
yet our \emph{learned} module stays $0.44$\,M ($3000$--$3300\times$ smaller than the trained parts of the large
trackers). Online throughput is unchanged from Table~\ref{tab:efficiency} because depth is read from the shared
offline cache; only the one-time depth precompute is ${\approx}4\times$ slower for the giant backbone.

$^{\S}$ $1.3$\,B frozen video-diffusion backbone, LoRA fine-tuned; predicts geometry directly (no DA3).
$^{\P}$ TAPIP3D and DELTA consume the \emph{same} shared DA3-g depth as our method, so their total includes
the $1.40$\,B backbone too --- making this a parameter-matched comparison ($1.43$--$1.49$\,B all round).
The DA3-g depth precompute is offline/one-time; the giant backbone has ${\approx}4\times$ the parameters of
DA3-l and precomputes correspondingly slower (${\approx}2.5$\,fps, estimated from the parameter ratio).
$^{\natural}$ TrackCraft3R could not be re-run on DA3-g ($12$\,GB OOM); shown for reference at its native size.
Component sizes: WAFT $64$\,M, DA3-g $1.40$\,B, DINOv3-S $22$\,M.}
\end{table}

\paragraph{Why TrackCraft3R is slow.}
TrackCraft3R~\cite{trackcraft3r} adapts Wan2.1-T2V-1.3B --- a video diffusion transformer ---
for 3D tracking via single-step regression.
Each \emph{inference window} (12 frames) requires four sequential GPU passes:
(i)~RGB VAE encode, (ii)~pointmap VAE encode, (iii)~DiT forward through 1.3\,B
parameters, and (iv)~two parallel VAE decodes (xyz $+$ visibility).
The DiT forward alone accounts for ${\approx}75$\,\% of the per-window cost (${\approx}15$--$18$\,s),
with the three VAE passes adding ${\approx}6$\,s; total ${\approx}21$\,s per window on our RTX\,4080.

Critically, each \emph{unique query anchor frame} requires its own window.
TAPVid-3D queries span many frames: a typical 150-frame pstudio clip produces
90--120 unique windows; a 300-frame adt clip produces 260--270 windows.
At 21\,s each, per-clip latency reaches ${\approx}32$\,min (pstudio) and ${\approx}95$\,min (adt),
giving an effective throughput of ${\approx}0.06$\,fps --- \textbf{${\approx}200\times$ slower}
than our baseline (12.4\,fps).

Our method achieves this throughput advantage because the Vision-Mamba-3 refiner processes
all $N$ tracks jointly in a single causal sweep per frame (no per-window restarts),
and the 0.44\,M-parameter SSM adds modest overhead over the frozen SEA-RAFT $+$ DA3-l baseline.
TAPIP3D (${\approx}3.1$\,fps) and SpatialTrackerV2 (${\approx}4.0$\,fps) are substantially
slower than our pipeline despite relying on simpler (non-diffusion) architectures,
indicating that their multi-frame windowing and cost-volume computations are the bottleneck.
Combined with the accuracy advantage on the absolute metric
(Table~\ref{tab:abs}: mean metric-AJ $0.256$ vs $0.020$ for TrackCraft3R across all three subsets;
see \S\ref{sec:trackcraft3r}),
our pipeline is competitive on both quality and efficiency axes.
